# The values that the app reads, each under a comment that says what goes
# there. This file stays out of git; .env.example has the same keys without
# values. Every value ends up in the app, where anyone can read it, so never
# put a secret here. An empty value counts as missing.
#
# Write a value as it is, or in double quotes if it has a space, a # or a quote.

# The public API key of the App Store app in RevenueCat, which starts with appl_
REVENUECAT_APPLE_KEY=

# The public API key of the Play Store app in RevenueCat, which starts with goog_
REVENUECAT_GOOGLE_KEY=

REVENUECAT_TEST_KEY=

PAYWALL_PRIVACY_URL=

=== AGENTS.md (role:app_entry) ===
# AGENTS.md

This guide tells coding agents what the code of this Flutter app does not show. Setup that needs a person or an account is in [README.md](README.md). When you change what a section describes, change the section too.

## App entry

The app is plain Flutter code that nothing regenerates. Change it by hand, unless a section below says what writes a file.

- `main()` in `lib/main.dart` initializes the binding, awaits `bootstrap()` and calls `runApp()`, in this order. Start-up code goes into `bootstrap()`.
- `bootstrap()` in `lib/bootstrap.dart` runs before the first frame, in four phases: what must come before anything else, then the platform services, then the services of the app, then what needs them. Put a new statement into its phase, and import neither `package:flutter/material.dart` nor `package:flutter/cupertino.dart` there.
- Put code that features share into `lib/core/<concern>/`, one directory for each concern. Put a feature, with its screens and their state, into `lib/features/<feature>/`.
- The root of the app asks the system for the icons of the status bar that suit the theme, around every screen, with `StatusBarIcons` of `lib/core/app/status_bar_icons.dart`: dark icons on a light theme, light ones on a dark theme. A screen without an app bar needs no `AnnotatedRegion` for them. Add one only to a screen whose top has another brightness than the theme, such as a picture behind the status bar.

- The section "Finish the setup" of the README lists the steps of the setup that no command can do, such as a step in the web console of a service that the app uses. Only the developer of the app does them: when a task depends on one of them, point the developer to that section, and do not try to do the step yourself.

After a change, run:

```bash
dart format lib test
flutter analyze
flutter test
```

Format only `lib/` and `test/`: `dart format .` also formats what Flutter writes into `build/`. The app as generated is formatted, `flutter analyze` finds no issue in it and `flutter test` passes.

- `App` in `lib/app.dart` is the root widget of the app and creates its one `MaterialApp`. Create no other in `lib/`: every screen is below it.
- The `theme`, `darkTheme`, `themeMode`, `locale` and localizations of the app go into the arguments of that `MaterialApp`.
- Tests mirror `lib/`: the test of `lib/<path>.dart` is `test/<path>_test.dart`.
- The app has only an Android and an iOS project, in `android/` and `ios/`.
- The entitlements of the iOS app, such as Push Notifications or Sign in with Apple, are in `ios/Runner/Runner.entitlements`. Every build configuration of the Runner target signs with this file, even while it is empty. The Signing & Capabilities tab of Xcode edits the same file.

## Analytics

- Record what users do through `AnalyticsService` of `lib/core/analytics/analytics_service.dart`, never through the SDK of a provider. `createAnalyticsService()` returns the one service of the app. Call it where the state of a screen is created, or, with a DI container in the app, take the service from the container instead.
- The service never fails, and you need not await a call: in debug mode it prints the failure of a provider.
- A new provider implements `AnalyticsService`. The function that creates it gets an entry in `_analyticsServices` in that file, `?_createAlone('<name>', <function>)`, or, if it starts asynchronously, one in `initAnalytics()`, which the file has once a provider needs it.

With `firebase_analytics`:

- Screen views need no code: the router calls `logFirebaseScreenView()` with each screen that the user sees, and it logs the screen under the name of its route.

## Authentication

- `appSession` in `lib/core/auth/app_session.dart` tells who uses the app, and knows it before the first frame. Its `value` is a `SignedOutSession`, an `AnonymousSession` or an `AccountSession`, and `appSession.value.uid` is the id of the user. Sign in, sign up, sign out and delete the account only through `appSession`. The app has one, so create no other `AppSessionController`.
- A call of `appSession` fails only with an `AuthFailure`. Catch it and show a text of the app for its `reason`. Show its `developerHint` in debug mode only. After `recentSignInRequired`, tell the user to sign out and sign in again.
- `authMode` in that file says who may use the app without an account. It gates nothing on its own. What keeps a user from a screen is a guard of the routes. One that reads `appSession.allowsApp` stands before the whole app, and one that reads `appSession.hasAccount` stands only before the routes that it lists, the screens that need an account. In an app without such a guard every screen is open, whatever the mode, and that includes a screen that needs an account. A screen does not redirect itself.
- When an anonymous user signs in to an account that exists already, `takeGuestData()` in `lib/core/auth/guest_data.dart` says what moves from that user to the account. As generated it moves nothing: write there what the app keeps under the id of a user. Call no method of `appSession` in it, since the sign-in that calls it would wait for that call.
- `appSignInMethods` in `lib/core/auth/sign_in_methods.dart` lists the ways to sign in with an identity provider that the modules of the app add, and is empty without them. A screen of sign-in shows a button for each `SignInMethod` whose `isAvailable` is true, with its `label(context)` and its `icon`.
- On a tap, the state of the screen awaits `credential()` of the way to sign in and gives the credential to `appSession.signInWith`. `credential()` returns `null` when the user closed the sheet of the identity provider, which is no failure: the screen goes back to how it was. Call `credential()` from the state of a screen, never within a call of `appSession`.
- `appSession.signInWith` gives an anonymous user the identity with the same id, or signs in to the account that has the identity already, and `takeGuestData()` moves what the anonymous user had. `appSession.deleteAccount()` asks a way to sign in with `revokesOnDeletion` for a credential again itself, and deletes nothing when the user closes its sheet.
- `AccountSession.issuers` lists the identity providers of an account, each the `issuer` of a `SignInMethod`.
- A new provider implements `AuthService` of `lib/core/auth/auth_service.dart`, and only the file of that implementation imports it. The code of the app imports `lib/core/auth/app_session.dart`, which exports `AuthFailure`, `AuthFailureReason`, `SignInCredential` and `IdTokenCredential`, and calls neither `initAuth()` nor `createAuthService()` nor `appSession.start()`.

With `firebase_auth`:

- `FirebaseAuthService` in `lib/core/auth/firebase_auth_service.dart` implements `AuthService` on `FirebaseAuth.instance`. In `lib/`, only that file imports the package: other code signs in through `appSession`.
- That file gives each code of Firebase its `AuthFailureReason`, in `_failureOf()`. A code that it does not know becomes `unknown`: give it a reason there.
- The ways to sign in are enabled in the Firebase project, not in the code. A call that fails with `notConfigured` tells in its `developerHint` how, and so does the section Firebase Authentication of `README.md`.
- `tool/enable_firebase_sign_in.dart` enables them with the Firebase CLI. It changes the Firebase project and needs a Firebase account: run it only when asked.

- For Google sign-in, `tool/enable_firebase_sign_in.dart` also registers the SHA of the Android debug key of the machine in the Firebase project, downloads the Firebase files of the app again and writes the URL scheme of the iOS app into `ios/Runner/Info.plist`. After `flutterfire configure` for another project, it has to run again: run it only when asked.
- Apple is enabled in the Firebase console by hand, with the private key of Apple that deleting an account needs. That key is a secret: never put it into the app or its repository.

## Crash reporting

- `installCrashReporting()` of `lib/core/crash_reporting/crash_reporter.dart`, which `bootstrap()` calls, reports the errors of the main isolate that nothing handles, through `FlutterError.onError` and `PlatformDispatcher.instance.onError`. Set these two nowhere else.
- Report an error that the code catches with `recordError()` of `CrashReporter`. `createCrashReporter()` returns the one reporter of the app, which reports to every provider and never fails. Call it where the state of a screen is created, or, with a DI container in the app, take the reporter from the container instead.
- A new provider implements `CrashReporter`. The function that creates it gets an entry in `_crashReporters` in that file, `?_createAlone('<name>', <function>)`, or, if it starts asynchronously, one in `initCrashReporting()`, which the file has once a provider needs it.

With `firebase_crashlytics`:

- Report through `CrashReporter`, not through `FirebaseCrashlytics.instance`.
- After `flutterfire configure` runs on macOS, `flutter build ipa` needs the fix of the section Crashlytics of `README.md`.

## Dependency injection

- A service is a type, usually an interface, and a top-level function that creates it from the services it takes as parameters. `registerDependencies()` in `lib/core/di/dependencies.dart` registers every service, and `bootstrap()` awaits it: a new service gets its line there.
- Only the composition file of a feature, `lib/features/<feature>/<feature>_composition.dart`, calls `resolve<T>()` of `lib/core/di/service_locator.dart`, to create what the screens of the feature need, such as the object that holds their state.
- A value known only at run time, such as an id, is a parameter of a function of the composition file, not a service.

With `get_it`:

- `registerDependencies()` registers the services in `getIt`, a local name for `GetIt.instance`, with `registerLazySingleton`, `registerSingleton` or `registerFactory`. The function that creates a service gets each service it takes as `getIt<Type>()`, and one that disposes of it goes into `dispose:`.
- A singleton created asynchronously takes `registerSingletonAsync`, and one that waits for such a service `registerSingletonWithDependencies`. `await getIt.allReady()` then ends the function.
- No other file in `lib/` imports `get_it`. A test may import it to register its fakes in `GetIt.instance`.

## Events

- An event is a class that extends `AppEvent` of `lib/core/events/communication_service.dart`: `fire()` of `CommunicationService` sends it, and `on<T>()` listens to its type.
- The app has one `CommunicationService`, which `createCommunicationService()` returns. Call it where the state of a screen is created, or, with a DI container in the app, take the service from the container instead. Create no other from its implementation: its listeners would get none of these events.
- Use events between parts of the app that do not import each other.

With `event_bus`:

- A listener gets an event once the code that fired it reaches its next `await` or returns, not inside `fire()`.

## Firebase

- `bootstrap()` initializes Firebase with `DefaultFirebaseOptions` of `lib/firebase_options.dart`. That file is a placeholder until `flutterfire configure` writes it, and the app stops at start-up with an `UnsupportedError` until then. Do not write it by hand.
- `flutterfire configure` needs a Firebase account and someone to choose the project: run it only when asked, with the command of the section Firebase of `README.md`.
- A test that runs `main()` or `bootstrap()` starts Firebase, which needs the options of `flutterfire configure` and a mock of its platform side. Prefer a test of a widget, which needs neither.

## Google sign-in

With `google_sign_in`:

- `googleCredential()` in `lib/core/google_sign_in/google_sign_in.dart` is the function of the way to sign in with Google in `appSignInMethods`, on Android and iOS. In `lib/`, only that file imports the package. It starts the package once with `GoogleSignIn.instance.initialize()`, without ids, so that the package reads the ids of the clients of Google from the files of the app, and then calls `GoogleSignIn.instance.authenticate()`. It returns an `IdTokenCredential` with the `idToken` of the account and no `rawNonce`.
- A closed sheet, `GoogleSignInExceptionCode.canceled`, returns `null`. These fail with `notConfigured`: `clientConfigurationError`, `providerConfigurationError`, and an `unknownError` whose description has the code `28444`, which Credential Manager answers when the Google project does not know the key that signed the Android app. Any other error fails with `unknown`. On iOS, an app without its iOS client of Google or its URL scheme stops at the sign-in: the Google Sign-In SDK throws a native exception there, which the package does not catch.
- `GoogleSignInIcon` shows the logo of Google, `lib/core/google_sign_in/assets/google_logo.png`, which `pubspec.yaml` declares among the assets of the app, at the size of the `IconTheme` around it. The branding guidelines of Google allow no other colours or shape of the logo.
- In a test, the package asks `GoogleSignInPlatform.instance` of google_sign_in_platform_interface, which the test replaces with a subclass of `GoogleSignInPlatform` that answers `init` and `authenticate`, before the first call of `googleCredential()`: the app starts the package once in a test file.

## Home

- `HomeScreen` in `lib/features/home/home_screen.dart`, the route `home.home` at `/home`, is a welcome to the developer of the app: it names the app and lists what to do next. Replace its content with the first screen of the app, and keep the route while the app starts there.
- The screen shows the image `lib/features/home/assets/smf_mark.png`, which `pubspec.yaml` declares among the assets of the app. Flutter takes the files of the same name in `2.0x/` and `3.0x/` next to it for a screen with more pixels. Once no screen shows the image, remove the files and their line in `pubspec.yaml`.

## Layout

- `AppShell` of `lib/core/layout/app_shell.dart` only shows the main navigation around the screen of the selected destination. The router creates it, with its `destinations`, `currentIndex`, `onSelect` and `body`, and keeps the stack of each destination, so keep these parameters. Without destinations, the router creates no `AppShell`.
- A destination is a `Destination` in `appDestinations` of `lib/core/layout/destination.dart`, an icon and a label, for a top-level route that needs no values. The routes below it stay in its stack, and every other top-level route shows over the main navigation.
- The label of a `Destination` is a top-level function that takes a `BuildContext` and returns the text, so that `appDestinations` stays `const`. Show a label with `destination.label(context)` in the `build` of the widget that shows it, and do not keep what it returned: the text follows the language of the app.
- To add a destination, add its `Destination` to `appDestinations` with a function for its label, and give the router a branch for it at the same index.
- `go()` to a location in the main navigation selects its destination. From a page shown over the main navigation, `push()` and `replace()` of such a location throw a `StateError`: use `go()`.

- `AppShell` draws the bar itself, as `_TabBar` with a `_Tab` for each destination, and hides it while the app has fewer than two destinations. Keep to five destinations, the most that fit the bar on a phone.
- The bar is not the navigation bar of Material, but it takes its colours where that bar does, from `NavigationBarTheme.of(context)`: `iconTheme` gives the colour of the icon and the label of the selected tab and of the others, and `backgroundColor` that of the bar. For a colour that the theme does not set, the bar reads the `colorScheme`: `primary` for the selected tab, `onSurfaceVariant` for the others and `surface` for the bar.
- A `_Tab` is one node for a screen reader, with the label of its destination. What you add to a tab, such as a badge, is not read unless the `Semantics` of the tab says it.

## Localization

- Show the user no text but the name of the app from a string literal. Read each text as `context.l10n.<name>`, with `lib/core/l10n/l10n.dart` imported, where `context` is a `BuildContext` below the root of the app. Such an expression is no constant, so the widget around it cannot be `const`.
- `lib/core/l10n/app_locale.dart` has `appLocales`, the languages of the app, and `appLocale`, the one that the user chose. Change the language only with `appLocale.choose(locale)`, which also saves the choice in the preferences, and write nothing under the key `localization.locale` yourself. The root `MaterialApp` gets its `locale` and its `supportedLocales` from that file: leave both as they are.
- Every delegate among the `localizationsDelegates` of the root has to support each language of `appLocales`. A new language, one in which Flutter has the texts of its own widgets, goes into `appLocales`, into `CFBundleLocalizations` in the Info.plist of the iOS app, and into the texts of the app.

- A new language also needs its name in `_names` in `lib/core/l10n/language_setting.dart`: the setting of the language on the settings screen shows a language by that name, and by its code without one. Each name there is a string literal, the name of the language in that language, which is the same whatever language the app is in.

With `gen-l10n` of Flutter:

- The texts of the app are in the ARB files of `lib/l10n`. `lib/l10n/app_en.arb` has every text in English, and `lib/l10n/app_<code>.arb` the translations into another language. A text that such a file lacks reads in English there.
- To add a text, add it to `lib/l10n/app_en.arb` under a name in lowerCamelCase, and its translations under the same name to the other files.
- After every change of the ARB files, a new file included, run `flutter gen-l10n`, which generates `context.l10n.<name>`. Do not edit `lib/l10n/app_localizations*.dart`: `gen-l10n` writes those files from the ARB files, as `l10n.yaml` says.
- Write an apostrophe in a text once: `l10n.yaml` turns the escapes of `gen-l10n` off (`use-escaping: false`).

## Notifications

- `appNotifications` in `lib/core/notifications/app_notifications.dart` shows the notifications of the app. `show()` shows one now, `schedule(notification, at: time)` at about that time, not to the minute, even if the app is closed by then. `cancel()` and `cancelAll()` remove them, and `scheduled()` lists those still to come, those of an owner too. A notification is an `AppNotification` with an `id`, a `title`, a `body` and `data`, and one with the id of a notification that is shown or scheduled takes its place. Give it an id from 0 up: a negative id is for a notification with an owner, which names it under `notificationOwnerKey` in its `data`, and whose taps go to `tapsOf(owner)` and `launchTapOf(owner)`.
- `appNotifications.permission` is `granted`, `denied` or `notAsked`. Ask with `appNotifications.request()` where the user turns on something that notifies, so that the question explains itself. Where the system has no question to show, as on Android before 13, the request opens its settings instead. After `denied` the system may not ask again (iOS asks only once, and Android stops after a second no), so open its settings with `appNotifications.openSettings()`. The app remembers that it asked under `notifications.asked`, so write nothing under that key.
- A tap on a notification reaches `appNotifications.taps` with the `data` of the notification, and the tap that launched the app is `appNotifications.launchTap`. Handle both once the first screen is shown, and navigate as the rest of the app does. A notification runs no code of the app when it shows.
- On Android a notification shows `android/app/src/main/res/drawable/ic_notification.xml` as its small icon. Change the drawing there and keep the name of the file.
- A new provider implements `NotificationsService` of `lib/core/notifications/notifications_service.dart`, and only the files of that implementation import it. The code of the app imports `lib/core/notifications/app_notifications.dart`, which exports `AppNotification` and `NotificationTap`, and calls neither `initNotifications()` nor `createNotificationsService()` nor `appNotifications.start()`.

- `NotificationsSetting` in `lib/core/notifications/notifications_setting.dart`, an entry of the settings screen, shows whether the app may show notifications. A tap asks while the app has not asked, and opens the settings of the system after that.

- The onboarding asks for the permission on a page of its own, with the functions of `lib/core/notifications/notifications_step.dart`. A user who skips the onboarding does not see that page, so code that needs notifications calls `appNotifications.request()` too.

With `flutter_local_notifications`:

- `LocalNotificationsService` in `lib/core/notifications/local_notifications_service.dart` implements `NotificationsService` on `FlutterLocalNotificationsPlugin`. In `lib/`, only that file imports the package: other code shows notifications through `appNotifications`.
- Every notification goes to the channel `notificationChannelId` with the details in `_details` of that file. For another channel, such as one with another sound, create it with `createNotificationChannel()` of `AndroidFlutterLocalNotificationsPlugin` in `openLocalNotifications()`, and show a notification in it with details of its own.
- A scheduled notification uses an inexact alarm (`AndroidScheduleMode.inexactAllowWhileIdle`). Exact alarms need the permission `SCHEDULE_EXACT_ALARM`, which Google Play allows only for an app whose main feature needs them: change the mode only when asked.
- `android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml` declares the receivers of the package, `android/app/build.gradle.kts` turns on core library desugaring, and `ios/Runner/AppDelegate.swift` sets the delegate of the notification center. Keep them: scheduled notifications, the Android build and the taps on iOS need them.
- The package has no platform side for tests. A test that runs `bootstrap()` answers the method channel `dexterous.com/flutter/local_notifications` itself, with `setMockMethodCallHandler` of `TestDefaultBinaryMessengerBinding.instance.defaultBinaryMessenger`, after `AndroidFlutterLocalNotificationsPlugin.registerWith()`.

## Onboarding

- On the first launch, the guard `onboarding.firstRun` of `routeGuards` shows `OnboardingScreen` of `lib/features/onboarding/onboarding_screen.dart`, the route `onboarding.onboarding` at `/onboarding`, in place of every other screen. So no code navigates to the onboarding or away from it. Once the onboarding is finished, a navigation to that route, such as a link, shows the screen that the app starts on, or the `redirectTo` of another guard while that one does not allow.
- The pages of the onboarding are the list that `onboardingPages()` returns in `lib/features/onboarding/onboarding_pages.dart`, in the order the user goes through them. To add or reorder a page, change that list. A page is any widget, such as an `OnboardingPage` with a `symbol` or an `icon` for its cell. The screen keeps Skip above the pages, and below them a mark for each page and its button, which is Next and, on the last page, Get started.
- Before the last page of that list, the screen shows the steps of `appOnboardingSteps` in `lib/core/onboarding/onboarding_steps.dart`, each on an `OnboardingPage` with the icon of the step. On the page of a step, the button below the pages is the action of the step. It spins while its `run` runs, or is disabled then in an app that asks for less motion. The `later` of the step is a second button below it, and Skip skips the steps too.
- The pages animate when they are first shown and while they turn, and each of these animations ends. Keep it so in a page of your own: a test that waits for the app to settle with `pumpAndSettle()` never ends on a screen that animates without end.
- `onboardingStatus` in `lib/features/onboarding/onboarding_status.dart` has what the guard reads, and saves it in the preferences under `onboarding.completed`. Its `complete()`, which Skip and the button on the last page call, finishes the onboarding, and its `restart()` starts it again. Nothing else shows the onboarding again: call `restart()` where the user asks to see it, and do not navigate. The router shows the onboarding and, once it is finished, the screen that the user was on, or the screen below the pages pushed over it.
- In a test that starts the app to check another screen, call `onboardingStatus.complete()` before the app starts. It saves nothing then, and the app does not show the onboarding.

## Onboarding screen

- The onboarding shows the steps of `appOnboardingSteps` in `lib/core/onboarding/onboarding_steps.dart`, each on a page of its own, in the order of the list. A step is an `OnboardingStep`: an `icon`, a `title`, a `text`, the label of its `action` and the function `run` that the action calls. Its `later` is the label of a button that goes on without calling `run`, and the onboarding leaves the step out when its `showIf` returns `false` as the onboarding opens.
- To add a step, add an `OnboardingStep` to `appOnboardingSteps`. Its labels, `run` and `showIf` are top-level functions, so that the list stays `const`: a label takes a `BuildContext` and returns the text, `run` takes the `BuildContext` of the onboarding screen and returns a `Future`, and `showIf` returns a `bool` at once. The onboarding goes on once the future of `run` completes. A `run` that shows a screen of its own over the onboarding pushes it with `Navigator.of(context, rootNavigator: true)` and completes once that screen is closed. Show a label with `step.title(context)` in the `build` of the widget that shows it, and do not keep what it returned: the text follows the language of the app.
- Skipping the onboarding skips its steps. So the app does not rely on `run` alone for what a step does, such as asking for a permission: the code that needs it calls the same function too.

## Paywall

- The paywall is `PaywallScreen`, the route `paywall.paywall` at `/paywall`, in `lib/features/paywall/`. The screen of the subscription is `SubscriptionScreen`, the route `paywall.subscription` at `/paywall/subscription`, which asks for the condition `PurchasesRole.premium`. The guard `paywall.hasPremium` of `routeGuards` stands for it, with `paywallHasPremium()` of `lib/features/paywall/paywall_guards.dart`, which returns `appPurchases.hasPremium`.
- No screen of the paywall closes itself or navigates after a purchase or a restore. The access changes, and the router closes the paywall and shows the route that was asked for. To sell from a button, navigate to a route that needs premium access, such as `context.nav.paywall.subscription().push<void>()`. For a user without the access, the router opens the paywall over the page first. A route of your own needs the access when a module lists `PurchasesRole.premium` in its `conditions`, or when its full name is among the `routes` of `paywall.hasPremium` in `routeGuards`.
- Never push `/paywall` for a user with premium access: the flow of the guard is over, so the router shows the screen that the app starts on.
- The look and the state are in different files. The look is `PaywallView` of `lib/features/paywall/paywall_view.dart` and `SubscriptionView` of `lib/features/paywall/subscription_view.dart`, with the widgets of `lib/features/paywall/paywall_widgets.dart` and the card of `lib/features/paywall/paywall_card.dart`. They are plain Flutter widgets: a view gets the state and callbacks, and imports nothing that manages state. Its state is a `PaywallState` of `lib/features/paywall/paywall_state.dart`, which stays busy after a purchase or a restore for as long as the user has premium access.
- The benefits on the paywall are texts of the module, `benefitAll`, `benefitNew` and `benefitSupport`: change them to what your premium access gives. With localization, `lib/features/paywall/paywall_view.dart` reads them as `context.l10n.paywallBenefitAll` and so on: change them where the provider of the localization keeps the texts of the app, in each language. Without it, they are English texts in that file.
- A plan gets its name, the suffix of its price and the length of its intro offer in `lib/features/paywall/paywall_texts.dart`, from the `period` of the plan and of its `introOffer`. A text has no parameters, so the file has a text for each period that the stores have, and the price is the one that the store gives. A failure gets its text in `failureTextOf()` there, which has a text for every `PurchasesFailureReason`, and `PaywallMessage` shows the `developerHint` of a failure in debug mode only.
- `lib/features/paywall/paywall_links.dart` reads the addresses of the terms of use and of the privacy policy from `PAYWALL_TERMS_URL` and `PAYWALL_PRIVACY_URL` of `.env`, and opens a link with `url_launcher`. While a value is empty, the paywall leaves its link out, but for the terms of use on iOS, which lead to the standard license agreement of Apple, and a debug build names the key in a note for the developer.
- In an app with sign-in, a user may buy before signing in. A sign-up keeps the purchase in the new account, while a sign-in to an account that exists already leaves it with the user of the device until a restore.
- While a purchase is on its way, and after one that gave premium access, the button spins, and so does the link of a restore. A widget test that waits with `pumpAndSettle()` returns only once the router closed the paywall, so until then it shows frames with `pump()`.

- The state of each screen is a cubit in a file next to it: `PaywallCubit` and `SubscriptionCubit`. The screen provides its cubit and builds its view from the state. A cubit takes the purchases through its constructor, so a test creates one with an `AppPurchasesController` of its own. `lib/features/paywall/paywall_composition.dart` creates the cubits with `appPurchases`.
- A widget reads the access of the user with `context.watch<PremiumCubit>().state`, an `AppAccess`. The root of the app provides the `PremiumCubit` of `lib/features/paywall/premium_cubit.dart`, which follows `appPurchases`.

## Preferences

- `AppPreferences` in `lib/core/preferences/app_preferences.dart` remembers the settings of the app between its launches, such as the theme mode. It is not encrypted: never save a token, a password, an API key or an encryption key in it.
- To remember a setting, write a function that takes the `AppPreferences` and add it to `_restorers` in that file. In it, read the setting, put it into the state that the widgets listen to, and keep the preferences in that state for its writes. The function awaits nothing, and keeps the current value when nothing is saved.
- Name a key `<owner id>.<setting>`, such as `theme.mode`, where the owner is the feature or the concern whose code keeps the setting.
- In the code of the app, do not call `createAppPreferences()` or `initPreferences()`. `bootstrap()` opens the preferences, and code gets them as the argument of its function in `_restorers`, or, with a DI container in the app, from the container. Widgets use the state and do not touch the preferences. A test may call both: `initPreferences()` opens the preferences again, as the next launch of the app does, and `createAppPreferences()` returns them.

With `shared_preferences`:

- In `lib/`, only `lib/core/preferences/shared_app_preferences.dart` imports the package. Other code uses `AppPreferences`.
- A number is read only as the type that it was saved as: `getDouble()` returns `null` for a key with an `int`, and `getInt()` for a key with a `double`.
- In a test that runs `bootstrap()`, first set `SharedPreferencesAsyncPlatform.instance = InMemorySharedPreferencesAsync.empty()`. Without it, opening the preferences throws. Both names are from the package `shared_preferences_platform_interface`: add it as a dev dependency.
- `pubspec.yaml` keeps `shared_preferences_android` at 2.5.2 or later. With 2.5.0 and 2.5.1, a release build fails to open the preferences on Android, because R8 renames Kotlin classes that the package looks up by name. Before you change the constraint, check that a release build of the app starts on Android.

## Purchases

- `appPurchases` in `lib/core/purchases/app_purchases.dart` tells whether the user has premium access, and knows it before the first frame. Its `value` is `NoAccess` or `PremiumAccess`, and `appPurchases.hasPremium` is the `ValueListenable<bool>` that widgets and guards of the routes read. Every plan gives the same premium access. The app has one controller, so create no other `AppPurchasesController`.
- `appPurchases.plans()` gives the plans to sell, each a `Plan` with its `price`, its `period` (`null` for a plan paid once) and its `introOffer`. `appPurchases.purchase(plan)` returns `false` when the user cancels, which is no failure, and `appPurchases.restore()` gives the user what the store account bought. A call fails only with a `PurchasesFailure`: show a text of the app for its `reason`, and its `developerHint` in debug mode only. After `pending`, the access arrives later without another call.
- A screen that sells shows the full price of each period most clearly, the length of a free trial and the price after it, that the plan renews until the user cancels, a way to restore purchases, links to the terms of use and the privacy policy, and a visible way to close it, as the App Store and Google Play ask.
- `PremiumAccess.manageUrl` is the page of the store where the user manages or cancels the plan. A screen only for users with premium access stands behind a guard of the routes that reads `appPurchases.hasPremium`. A screen does not redirect itself.
- While the provider lacks what it needs, such as a key, `value` stays `NoAccess` and every call fails with the reason `notConfigured`, whose `developerHint` names what is missing.
- A new provider implements `PurchasesService` of `lib/core/purchases/purchases_service.dart`, and only the files of that implementation import it. The code of the app imports `lib/core/purchases/app_purchases.dart`, which exports the types of the answers, and calls neither `initPurchases()` nor `createPurchasesService()` nor `appPurchases.start()`.

- The purchases belong to the user of `appSession`, by `appSession.value.uid`, and to the user of this device while nobody is signed in. A user who buys before signing up keeps the purchase in the new account, which takes it over from the user of this device. A user who signs in to an account that exists already leaves the purchases with the user of this device, and after a sign-out the user of this device is a new one, with nothing bought. A purchase then comes back only through a restore, which may also move purchases from another user, as the backend of the provider decides. The app does not restore on its own: call `appPurchases.restore()` where the user asks for it.

- `RestorePurchasesSetting` in `lib/core/purchases/restore_purchases_setting.dart`, an entry of the settings screen, restores purchases on a tap and shows what it found.

With `purchases_flutter`:

- `RevenueCatPurchasesService` in `lib/core/purchases/revenuecat_purchases_service.dart` implements `PurchasesService` on `Purchases`. In `lib/`, only that file imports the package: other code buys and restores through `appPurchases`.
- The premium access is the entitlement `premium` in RevenueCat, which every package of the current offering unlocks. The id of a `Plan` is the identifier of its package.
- `revenueCatKey()` chooses the key from `.env`: `REVENUECAT_TEST_KEY` only when `kDebugMode` is true and it is filled in, and otherwise `REVENUECAT_APPLE_KEY` on iOS and `REVENUECAT_GOOGLE_KEY` on Android. Keep that rule: RevenueCat stops a profile or release build that is configured with a key of the Test Store. A secret key (`sk_`) never goes into the app.
- Never call `Purchases` before `Purchases.configure()` has completed, or without a key: iOS stops the app. Every call of the service waits for its configuration, and without a key it fails with `notConfigured` and calls nothing.
- RevenueCat answers from a cache of the customer info. A grant made on the dashboard or with the API of RevenueCat shows after `Purchases.invalidateCustomerInfoCache()` and the next read of the customer info.
- The package has no platform side for tests. A test that runs `bootstrap()` answers the method channel `purchases_flutter` itself, with `setMockMethodCallHandler` of `TestDefaultBinaryMessengerBinding.instance.defaultBinaryMessenger`. The listeners and the last customer info of `Purchases` are static, so they last for a whole test file.

## Push messages

- `appPush` in `lib/core/push/app_push.dart` receives the push messages of the app. A message is a `PushMessage` with `data`, and with an `id`, a `title` and a `body` when it has them.
- `appPush.address` is the address of this device for the backend that sends the messages. It is `null` until the platform gives one. Listen to it and send each new address to the backend: it may come late, and it may change. Never wait for it before the first frame.
- `appPush.messages` tells of each message that arrives while the app is in the foreground. A message with a title or a body also shows as a notification there, which needs the permission of the notifications of the app. `appNotifications.request()` asks for it.
- A tap on a message reaches `appPush.taps`, and the message whose tap launched the app is `appPush.launchMessage`. Handle both once the first screen is shown, and navigate as the rest of the app does. Such a tap does not reach `appNotifications.taps`.
- A message with data and no title or body reaches the app only while it is in the foreground.
- A new provider implements `PushService` of `lib/core/push/push_service.dart`, and only the files of that implementation import it. The code of the app imports `lib/core/push/app_push.dart`, which exports `PushMessage`, and calls neither `initPush()` nor `createPushService()` nor `appPush.start()`.

With `firebase_messaging`:

- `FirebasePushService` in `lib/core/push/firebase_push_service.dart` implements `PushService` on `FirebaseMessaging`. In `lib/`, only that file imports the package: other code receives push messages through `appPush`.
- The address of the device is its registration token of Firebase Cloud Messaging, from `getToken()`, and a new token comes on `onTokenRefresh`. On iOS there is none until the token of the Apple Push Notification service comes, and the package tells the app nothing when it comes. So while something listens to `addressChanges`, the service reads that token with `getAPNSToken()` now and then, for at most two minutes, and then tells of the registration token. Keep that wait.
- On iOS, `createFirebasePushService()` calls `setForegroundNotificationPresentationOptions()` with an alert, a badge and a sound. Keep it: the package answers iOS for every notification in the foreground, those of `appNotifications` too, and without these options iOS shows none of them.
- Never call `requestPermission()` of the package: the permission to show notifications belongs to `appNotifications.request()`.
- `android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml` names the channel and the icon with which Android shows a message that arrives in the background, those of the notifications of the app. Change them together.
- The section Firebase Cloud Messaging of the README tells how to upload the key of the Apple Push Notification service and how to send this device a test message.
- The package has no platform side for tests. A test that runs `bootstrap()` answers the method channel `plugins.flutter.io/firebase_messaging` itself, with `setMockMethodCallHandler` of `TestDefaultBinaryMessengerBinding.instance.defaultBinaryMessenger`.

## Router

- Navigate with `context.nav.<feature>.<route>(...)`, never with the package of the router. It returns a `NavLink`, whose `go()`, `push<T>()` and `replace()` show the location. In `context.nav` both names are in lowerCamelCase; elsewhere `<feature>` is the id of the feature.
- A route is in three places, which agree on its name, `<feature>.<route>`, and on its path, which starts with `/<feature>`:
  1. its screen, a widget in `lib/features/<feature>/` whose `const` constructor takes each value of the route as a named parameter;
  2. `lib/core/router/navigation.dart`: its location class, which extends the sealed `AppLocation` with its `routeName`, its `path` and, below another route, its `parent`, and a method that returns its `NavLink` in the class of the routes of its feature. `AppNav` returns that class from a getter with the name of the feature, and both classes pass on the `BuildContext` that `NavLink` takes;
  3. the route itself, where the provider of the router declares its routes.
- A value of a route is a `String`, an `int`, a `double` or a `bool`, from a `:<name>` segment of its path or from the query.
- `appRouter` of `lib/core/router/app_router.dart` is the one router of the app: create no other.

- `routeGuards` in `lib/core/router/app_router.dart` lists the guards of the routes, each a `RouteGuard`, in the order the router asks them. A guard without `routes` is a gate over the whole app. The gates come first, and the first one whose `allows` is `false` decides: the router asks no guard after it. A guard with `routes` keeps the user only from the routes that it names there, each as `<feature>.<route>`, and comes after the gates.
- To keep the user from the rest of the app, add a gate to that list, never a redirect to the files of the router. Put it at the place among the gates where its screen comes on a first launch: a guard of the first launch goes before one that asks who the user is. Make its `redirectTo` a top-level route outside the main navigation. To keep the user from some routes only, add their names to the `routes` of the guard that stands for what they need, or add such a guard after the gates. Add the name of each route below such a route too: a route does not ask because the route above it does. The `routes` are outside the main navigation and outside every `flow`, and the screen that the app starts on is none of them.
- A feature changes the value of `allows` of its guard, and the router navigates when it does. While the `allows` of a gate is `false`, the router shows its `redirectTo` in place of the whole stack, whichever location was asked for. Once the gates allow, the router shows the location that was asked for in the meantime, such as a link, or else brings the user back to where they were. A gate with `resumes: false` does not bring the user back. When the `allows` of a guard with `resumes: false` turns `false`, the router forgets where the user was and what was asked for before, even while the `redirectTo` of another guard is shown. Once the gates allow, the user comes to a location that was asked for since then, or else to the screen that the app starts on. So after a sign-out the next user does not come to the screen of the last one.
- While the `allows` of a guard with `routes` is `false`, `go()`, `push()` or `replace()` of one of those routes opens its `redirectTo` over the page that the user is on, and the request waits. Back returns to that page, and a `push()` that waited completes with `null`. Once the value is `true`, the router closes the pages of the `flow` and does what was asked: `push()` shows the route over the page that the user was on and completes with the value of its page, `go()` shows it in place of the stack, and `replace()` in place of that page. When the value turns `false`, the router closes the pages of the `routes` with the pages over them. The user is then on the page below them, or on the screen that the app starts on when no page is below, as after `go()` to one of the `routes`.
- A screen neither closes itself nor navigates after it changed the value of `allows` of a guard: the router has done both by then. This goes for a screen of a `flow` that turned the value `true`, as after a sign-in, and for a screen of the `routes` that turned it `false`, as a screen with a sign-out button. A `pop()` after the change closes the page that is on top by then: the page that the user asked for, or the page below the one that the router closed, which may be the main navigation of the app.
- The `flow` of a guard is its `redirectTo` and the routes below it. These routes show only while a guard with that `flow` does not allow. At any other time the router shows the screen that the app starts on in their place, or the `redirectTo` of a gate whose `allows` is `false`. So no code navigates into the `flow` of a gate, and no screen of a `flow` navigates out of it once its guard allows: the router does. The first does not hold for a guard with `routes`. While it does not allow and every gate does, its `redirectTo` shows like any route, so a screen may open it with `push()`. `go()` to it from a screen takes the place of the stack, with no way back. A link to a route of its `flow` opens that route over the screen that the app starts on, so back leads into the app. Such a link is lost while a gate with another `flow` does not allow: once the gate allows, the user is on the screen that the app starts on. Inside a `flow`, navigate with `push()` or `replace()`. `go()` takes the place of the pages below the `flow`, and the request that waits is lost with them.

With `go_router`:

- `lib/core/router/app_router_factory.dart` has the one `GoRouter` of the app. A route is a `GoRoute` in its `routes`, or in the `routes` of its parent: `path` is the full path of a top-level route and the path from its parent otherwise, and `name` is `<feature>.<route>`. The values of a route come from `state.pathParameters` and `state.uri.queryParameters`. Parse one that is not a `String` with `tryParse`, and throw a `GoException` in the `redirect` of the route when a required value is missing or does not parse, so that the router shows its error screen.
- The app opens at `initialLocation`, and the route `/` leads to the same screen: change both together.
- To follow the screen that the user sees, add a function to `_screenListeners`.

- The destinations of the main navigation are the branches of the `StatefulShellRoute.indexedStack` that `_mainNavigation()` creates, in the order of `appDestinations` in `lib/core/layout/destination.dart`, which `AppShell` gets as its `destinations`. A new destination is a `StatefulShellBranch` there, with `observers: _observers()`, and a `Destination` at the same index of that list. A route outside the main navigation goes after `_mainNavigationRoute` in `routes`.
- The router calls `_mainNavigation()` again each time the main navigation leaves its pages, so that the main navigation comes back with each branch on its destination. Create what a branch has of its own, such as a key of its navigator, inside that function.

## Settings screen

- The settings screen is a list with an entry for each setting of the app. An entry is a widget in a file of the code that owns the setting, such as one in `lib/core/<concern>/` or `lib/features/<feature>/`, and not in the file of the screen. It reads and changes its setting itself.
- To add a setting, write such a widget with a `const` constructor that requires no arguments. Create it in the list of the screen, and import its file there with a prefix that no other import there has, because two entries may have the same class name.
- The screen puts each entry on a `Material`, sets its width and puts no limit on its height, so an entry can be a `ListTile` or a `Column` of them. Pass an entry no arguments: it gets its state from its owner.

- `SettingsScreen` in `lib/features/settings/settings_screen.dart`, the route `settings.settings` at `/settings`, is the settings screen. Below its title it shows the entries in one group, and the file imports the files of the entries as `entry0`, `entry1` and so on. While no module of the app has a setting, it shows a note for the developer of the app in place of the group: the first entry replaces that note.
- With a main navigation in the app, the screen is one of its destinations.
- Without a main navigation, no screen leads to the settings screen: open it with `context.nav.settings.settings().push<void>()`, which shows it on top of the current screen, so that its back button leads back. `go()` would replace the stack with the screen alone, and nothing would lead back.

## Sign in with Apple

With `sign_in_with_apple`:

- `appleCredential()` in `lib/core/apple_sign_in/apple_sign_in.dart` is the function of the way to sign in with Apple in `appSignInMethods`, which works on iOS only. In `lib/`, only that file imports the package. It asks `SignInWithApple.getAppleIDCredential()` with the SHA-256 hash of a nonce of `generateNonce()`, and returns an `IdTokenCredential` with that nonce as its `rawNonce` and the `authorizationCode` of the request.
- Apple gives `givenName` and `familyName` only the first time that a user signs in to the app. A later credential has none, so do not expect the name there.
- A closed sheet, `AuthorizationErrorCode.canceled`, returns `null`. `AuthorizationErrorCode.unknown`, which Apple answers when the app is signed without the entitlement `com.apple.developer.applesignin` of `ios/Runner/Runner.entitlements`, fails with `notConfigured`, and any other error with `unknown`.
- In a test, `appleCredential()` asks Apple only while `debugDefaultTargetPlatformOverride` is `TargetPlatform.iOS`. The package asks the platform on the method channel `com.aboutyou.dart_packages.sign_in_with_apple`, whose method `performAuthorizationRequest` a test answers with a map whose `type` is `appleid`, with `identityToken`, `authorizationCode`, `givenName` and `familyName`.

## Sign-in

- The sign-in is three screens in `lib/features/sign_in/`: `SignInScreen`, the route `sign_in.signIn` at `/sign_in`, and over it `SignUpScreen` at `/sign_in/sign_up` and `ResetPasswordScreen` at `/sign_in/reset_password`. Two guards of `routeGuards` show them, with the functions of `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_guards.dart`. `sign_in.gate` reads `appSession.allowsApp` and shows the sign-in in place of every other screen. `sign_in.hasAccount` reads `appSession.hasAccount` and shows it in place of a route that needs an account.
- A screen of the sign-in neither closes itself nor navigates once the user is signed in. It signs in or up through its state. The session changes then, and the router leaves the sign-in. While `authMode` is `AuthMode.required`, no code navigates to the sign-in either: the gate shows it. In the other modes, a screen that offers to sign in opens it with `context.nav.signIn.signIn().push<void>()`, so that back leads back.
- The look of a screen and its state are in different files. The look is `SignInView`, `SignUpView` and `ResetPasswordView`, each in a file of its name, such as `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_view.dart`. They share `AuthPage` of `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_page.dart`, which is the frame of a screen, and the fields and buttons of `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_widgets.dart`. All of them are plain Flutter widgets: a view gets the state of its call and callbacks, and imports nothing that manages state. The file of a screen, such as `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_screen.dart`, only gives its view the state.
- The fields, the buttons and the cards of the screens are those of the theme of the app. These files give a field no colour, border or padding, so for another look of a field, change the theme of the app. `OtherScreenAction`, the way from the sign-in to the sign-up and back, is a text button of the theme with one colour of its own, `colorScheme.secondary`.
- The title of a page and the label of each button are each a `WholeWords` of `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_widgets.dart`, not a `Text`. It breaks a text between its words and never inside one. Where the longest word would not fit, as on a narrow phone with a large text size, the text is only as large as that word allows. Use it for the label of a new button of the feature too.
- Above the form, the sign-in and the sign-up show a `SignInMethodButton` for each way to sign in of `appSignInMethods` that works on the platform, from `signInMethodsHere()` of `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_widgets.dart`. A module of a way to sign in adds its button, and this feature adds nothing for it. On a tap, `continueWith(method)` of the state calls `continueWithMethod()` of `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_state.dart`, which awaits `credential()` of the way to sign in and gives the credential to `appSession.signInWith`. These buttons are white with a grey hairline, with the label and the icon in black, in both themes, so that a logo in its own colours stays on white.
- That state is an `AuthActionState` or a `ResetPasswordState` of `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_state.dart`. It stays busy once a sign-in or a sign-up succeeded, for as long as the session has the account, since the router leaves the screen then. When the session has no account again before that, the form is back.
- A failure gets its text in `authFailureText()` of `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_widgets.dart`, which has a text for every `AuthFailureReason`. `FailureMessage` shows that text, and the `developerHint` of the failure in debug mode only.
- While a call is on its way, `SubmitButton` spins. A widget test that waits with `pumpAndSettle()` returns only once the call has ended, so until then it shows frames with `pump()`.
- The screen of the account is `AccountScreen`, the route `sign_in.account` at `/sign_in/account`, with its look in `AccountView` of `lib/features/sign_in/account_view.dart`. The route needs an account, so for a user without one the router opens the sign-in over the page first. `AccountSetting` of `lib/features/sign_in/account_setting.dart` is the entry of the account on the settings screen. It opens the screen with `context.nav.signIn.account().push<void>()`. In an app without a main navigation, no screen leads to the settings screen: a screen of the app opens it through `context.nav`, or opens the account with that same call.
- The screen of the account signs out and deletes the account through its state, an `AccountState`. It neither closes itself nor navigates after that: the router closes its page, or shows the sign-in in place of every screen. The state stays busy after a call that succeeded for as long as the session has no account. `confirmDeleteAccount()` asks in a sheet first, and the screen deletes the account only once that sheet is closed. A failed deletion gets its text in `deletionFailureText()` of `lib/features/sign_in/account_view.dart`. `DestructiveAction` is a text button of the theme in the colour `colorScheme.error`.

- The state of each screen is a cubit in a file next to it: `SignInCubit`, `SignUpCubit`, `ResetPasswordCubit` and `AccountCubit`. The screen provides its cubit and builds its view from the state. A cubit takes the session through its constructor, so a test creates one with an `AppSessionController` of its own. `lib/features/sign_in/sign_in_composition.dart` creates the cubits with `appSession`, and apart from the file of the guards, no other file of the feature names `appSession`.
- A widget reads who uses the app with `context.watch<SessionCubit>().state`, an `AppSession`. The root of the app provides the `SessionCubit` of `lib/features/sign_in/session_cubit.dart`, which follows `appSession`.

## State management

With `flutter_bloc`:

- The state of a screen is a `Cubit` in the directory of its feature. The screen provides it with a `BlocProvider` around its content. In a `BlocBuilder` there, it passes the state on to its view as plain values, and the methods of the `Cubit` as callbacks.
- The view is a widget in a file of its own that imports no `flutter_bloc`, so the look of a screen does not depend on what manages its state.
- When a screen is gone, its `BlocProvider` closes the `Cubit` that it created. For state that several screens read, put a `BlocProvider` around the root widget in `runApp()`, in `lib/main.dart`. A widget reads that `Cubit` with `context.watch`.
- After an `await`, a `Cubit` checks `isClosed` before it calls `emit()`. The user may have left the screen while the call was on its way, and `emit()` throws in a `Cubit` that is closed.
- A widget talks only to a `Cubit`, never to a service or a DI container. A `Cubit` takes what it needs through its constructor.

## Theme

- `createLightTheme(context)` and `createDarkTheme(context)` in `lib/core/theme/app_theme.dart` return the light and the dark theme of the app. Change the look there. Keep both functions and their `BuildContext` parameter, because the root of the app calls them each time it builds, for the `theme` and the `darkTheme` of the root `MaterialApp`. Leave those two arguments and its `themeMode` as they are.
- The theme mode is in `lib/core/theme/theme_mode.dart`. Change it only with `appThemeMode.choose(mode)`, which also saves it in the preferences, and write nothing under the key `theme.mode` yourself. A widget reads the mode with `AppThemeModeScope.of(context)` and rebuilds when it changes.
- Take the colours and the text styles of a screen from `Theme.of(context)`, not from constants, so that the screen follows the mode.

- `_themeOf` in `lib/core/theme/app_theme.dart` creates both themes: a Material 3 `ThemeData` with the colours of `_schemeOf`, the text styles of `_textThemeOf` and the themes of the components, such as `cardTheme` and `inputDecorationTheme`. Give that `ThemeData` what both themes share. For what differs between the two, use the `brightness` that `_themeOf` gets.
- A text field takes its border, its fill and its padding from `inputDecorationTheme`, in both themes. Give a field of a screen a decoration only for what that field alone has, such as its hint or an icon.
- `_schemeOf` has the colours of each theme. It sets most of them itself, and `ColorScheme.fromSeed` derives the others from `seedColor`. For another look, change the colours there. Keep the colour of what stands on a colour readable on it, such as `onPrimary` on `primary`.
- The font of the themes is the family `_fontFamily`, which `pubspec.yaml` declares with the files in `assets/fonts/geist/`, in the weights 400, 500 and 600. For another font, add its files, declare its family in `pubspec.yaml` and name it in `_fontFamily`. `assets/fonts/geist/OFL.txt` is the licence of the font, which the app bundles as an asset: keep it while the app has the font.
- Keep `brightness: brightness` in the colour scheme: `createLightTheme(context)` must return a light theme and `createDarkTheme(context)` a dark one.

## Values in .env

- Read a value of `.env` only through a static getter of `AppEnv` in `lib/core/env/app_env.dart`. The getter is the key in lowerCamelCase: `AppEnv.myServiceKey` for the key `MY_SERVICE_KEY`. It returns a `String?` that is `null` while the value is empty or missing, so handle `null`. Never read `.env` or `.env.example` from code.
- Every value ends up in the app, where anyone can read it. Never put a secret into `.env`, such as the secret key of a server, a password or a private key.
- `.env` stays out of git. A new key goes into both `.env.example` and `.env`, in UPPER_SNAKE_CASE with a comment line above it, and `AppEnv` gets a getter for it.

With `--dart-define-from-file`:

- Every command of the `flutter` tool that builds, runs or tests the app takes `--dart-define-from-file=.env`, such as `flutter run --dart-define-from-file=.env`, `flutter test` and `flutter build`. Without it, every getter of `AppEnv` is `null`.
- In VS Code, `.vscode/launch.json` passes it to Run and Debug (F5), to the Run and Debug links above `main()` and to the tests run from their links or from the Testing view, since its configuration is the template of every launch (`templateFor`). In Android Studio, `.run/main.run.xml` passes it only to the run configuration named after the app, not to a test that you run there.
- A command reads `.env` only when it starts. After a change of `.env`, stop `flutter run` and start it again: hot reload and hot restart keep the values that the app was built with.
- The getter of a new key in `lib/core/env/app_env.dart` reads `const String.fromEnvironment('MY_SERVICE_KEY')`, with the key in place of `MY_SERVICE_KEY`, and returns `null` for an empty value, as the other getters do.
