# my_app

A new Flutter project.

Generated with [Say My Frame](https://doc.saymyframe.com).

## Finish the setup

Once any commands of the sections below have run, the setup still needs these steps, which no command can do. [The guide](https://doc.saymyframe.com/setup?steps=app_entry.ios_app_id,purchases.store_agreements,purchases.store_products,firebase_analytics.google_analytics,firebase_auth.apple_provider,firebase_auth.apple_revocation,firebase_auth.google_release_sha,firebase_messaging.apns_key,revenuecat.entitlement_and_offering,revenuecat.api_keys,revenuecat.app_store_key,revenuecat.play_service_account,paywall.legal_pages) shows each one click by click:

1. In Xcode, sign the iOS app with your paid Apple Developer team
2. Set up payments in App Store Connect and Google Play Console
3. Create the products of your plans in App Store Connect and Google Play Console
4. Turn on Google Analytics in the Firebase project, unless it is on already
5. Turn on Sign in with Apple in Firebase Authentication
6. Give Firebase your Sign in with Apple key, so that deleting an account works
7. Before releasing on Google Play, give Firebase the SHA of your release keys
8. Give Firebase your Apple push key (APNs key)
9. Set up the entitlement premium and a current offering in RevenueCat
10. Copy the API keys of your RevenueCat apps into .env
11. Give RevenueCat your In-App Purchase key from App Store Connect
12. Give RevenueCat access to Google Play with a service account
13. Publish your terms of use and privacy policy and put their links into .env

## Run, test and build with .env

`AppEnv` in `lib/core/env/app_env.dart` reads each value of `.env` with `String.fromEnvironment`. The `flutter` tool reads `.env` when a command that builds, runs or tests the app starts, given `--dart-define-from-file=.env`, and compiles each value into the app. Without the argument, every getter of `AppEnv` is `null`, so give it to each such command:

```bash
flutter run --dart-define-from-file=.env
flutter test --dart-define-from-file=.env
flutter build apk --dart-define-from-file=.env
flutter build appbundle --dart-define-from-file=.env
flutter build ipa --dart-define-from-file=.env
```

In VS Code, `.vscode/launch.json` passes the argument to Run and Debug (F5), to the Run and Debug links above `main()`, and to the tests that you run from the links above them or from the Testing view: its configuration is the template of every launch of the app (`templateFor`). In Android Studio, `.run/main.run.xml` passes it to the run configuration named after the app, and a test that you run there gets no argument.

A command reads the values only when it starts: after you change `.env`, stop `flutter run` and start it again. Hot reload and hot restart keep the values that the app was built with.

To read a key of your own, give `AppEnv` a getter for it, as for the other keys:

```dart
static String? get myServiceKey {
  const value = String.fromEnvironment('MY_SERVICE_KEY');
  return value.isEmpty ? null : value;
}
```

The section "Values in .env" tells what goes into `.env` and lists the keys of the app.

## Firebase

The app uses [Firebase](https://firebase.google.com/docs/flutter/setup) through `firebase_core`: `bootstrap()` initializes it with the options of `DefaultFirebaseOptions` in `lib/firebase_options.dart`. `flutterfire configure` of the FlutterFire CLI writes these options when it registers the app in a Firebase project, together with `android/app/google-services.json`, `firebase.json` and, on macOS, `ios/Runner/GoogleService-Info.plist`. Until then, the options are a placeholder, and the app stops at start-up with an `UnsupportedError`.

To configure the app, or to configure it again, such as for another Firebase project or on another machine, run in its directory:

```bash
dart pub global activate flutterfire_cli 1.4.1
firebase login
flutterfire configure --platforms=android,ios --overwrite-firebase-options --ios-bundle-id=com.example.my-app --android-package-name=com.example.my_app
```

The command gives flutterfire the ids of the Android and iOS apps that SMF generated; if you change them, change them in the command too. `flutterfire configure` needs the [Firebase CLI](https://firebase.google.com/docs/cli), and changes the Xcode project only on macOS. The build phases that it adds to the Xcode project for some Firebase packages run `flutterfire` from `~/.pub-cache/bin`, so every machine that builds such an app for iOS needs the FlutterFire CLI activated too.

Configure the app with flutterfire_cli 1.4.1 or a later 1.x; if `dart pub global list` shows one already, skip its activation above.

## Firebase Authentication

The app signs in with [Firebase Authentication](https://firebase.google.com/docs/auth) through `firebase_auth`: `lib/core/auth/firebase_auth_service.dart` implements the sign-in service of the app on it.

The ways to sign in are enabled in the Firebase project, not in the code: Email/Password in every mode of the app, Anonymous when the mode of the app is `anonymous` (see the section Sign-in), and Google and Apple for the ways to sign in of the app. Until they are enabled, signing in and signing up fail with the reason `notConfigured`, and the `developerHint` of the failure tells how to enable them. In the mode `anonymous`, the app also starts without a user.

`smf create` asks to enable them right after `flutterfire configure` has configured the app. To enable them later, for another Firebase project, or after you changed the mode of the app to `anonymous`, run in the directory of the app:

```bash
dart tool/enable_firebase_sign_in.dart
```

The script reads the project from `firebase.json`, where `flutterfire configure` wrote it, and the mode from `authMode` in `lib/core/auth/app_session.dart`, and says which project and which mode it enables them for. It enables the methods with `firebase deploy --only auth` of the [Firebase CLI](https://firebase.google.com/docs/cli) 15.34.0 or later. It never disables a method, and it changes no file of the app but those that Google needs, as told below. `--project <id>` names another project, and the other arguments go to the Firebase CLI as they are. The script runs the Firebase CLI in a temporary directory, where an account that `firebase login:use` chose for the directory of the app does not apply. To name the account:

```bash
dart tool/enable_firebase_sign_in.dart --account <email>
```

For Google sign-in, the script also gives Google the name of the app and the email address of the account of the Firebase CLI for the consent screen that users see when they sign in. The address is the support email there. When the Firebase CLI runs as no user account, as with a service account, the script leaves Google out and says so: run it with `--account <email>`, or enable Google in the Firebase console.

The script sets up the rest of what Google needs only for the project that `firebase.json` names, not for one that `--project` names:

- It registers the SHA-1 and SHA-256 of the Android debug key of the machine that runs it, which Google uses to recognize the Android app. When the machine has no debug key yet, the script creates it, as the first debug build would. Every machine has a debug key of its own, so run the script on each machine that builds the app for debugging, or add the SHA of its key in the Firebase console, in the settings of the Android app.
- It downloads the Firebase files of the app again, such as `android/app/google-services.json` and `ios/Runner/GoogleService-Info.plist`, once they have the clients of Google, and writes them where `flutterfire configure` wrote them.
- It writes the URL scheme of the iOS app, `REVERSED_CLIENT_ID` of `ios/Runner/GoogleService-Info.plist`, into `CFBundleURLTypes` of `ios/Runner/Info.plist`. It adds the scheme and removes none: after a change of the project, remove the scheme of the former project there by hand.

Run the script again after `flutterfire configure` for another project.

A release of the Android app is signed with another key, whose SHA-1 and SHA-256 Google needs too: those of the upload key, and of the app signing key of Google Play, under Test and release > App integrity in the Play Console. Add each with the Firebase CLI, with the id of the Android app from `firebase.json`:

```bash
firebase apps:android:sha:create <appId> <sha>
```

The section Finish the setup lists this among the steps that you do by hand.

Apple is enabled in the Firebase console, which the Firebase CLI cannot do: add Apple under [Authentication > Sign-in method](https://console.firebase.google.com/project/_/authentication/providers). Deleting an account that signs in with Apple revokes the tokens that Apple gave the app, as the App Store asks of an app with Sign in with Apple (App Store Review Guideline 5.1.1(v)). Firebase does that with the Services ID and the OAuth code flow configuration, which you enter in the settings of the Apple provider there: your Apple team ID, and the Key ID and the private key of a key for Sign in with Apple. The private key is a secret: enter it in the console yourself, and never put it into the app or its repository. Until they are set, deleting an account that signs in with Apple fails with `notConfigured`, and the account stays. The section Finish the setup lists both, adding Apple and entering the key, among the steps that you do by hand.

You can also enable the methods in the Firebase console, under [Authentication > Sign-in method](https://console.firebase.google.com/project/_/authentication/providers) in the project that the app is configured for.

Firebase sends the message with which a user sets a new password from a template of the project: [Authentication > Templates](https://console.firebase.google.com/project/_/authentication/emails).

When an anonymous user signs in to an account that exists already, the anonymous user stays in the project, and nobody can sign in as that user again. A project that is upgraded to Firebase Authentication with Identity Platform can delete anonymous users that are older than 30 days automatically. See [Automatic clean-up](https://firebase.google.com/docs/auth/android/anonymous-auth#auto-cleanup) in the documentation of Firebase.

## Crashlytics

The app reports its errors to [Firebase Crashlytics](https://firebase.google.com/docs/crashlytics) through `firebase_crashlytics` once Firebase is configured. On macOS, `flutterfire configure` adds a build phase to the Xcode project that uploads the debug symbols of the app with the upload script of Crashlytics. The phase that flutterfire_cli 1.4.0 adds does not find the script where Flutter puts the Swift packages of the app, so with Swift Package Manager `flutter run` and `flutter build ios` fail in that phase. Configuring the app again on macOS with flutterfire_cli 1.4.1 or a later 1.x replaces the phase.

`flutter build ipa` needs one more change on macOS. The phase of flutterfire_cli 1.4.1 looks for the upload script in the build directory of Xcode, which is `build/ios` for `flutter run` and `flutter build ios` but not for the archive of `flutter build ipa`. So SMF points the phase at `build/ios/SourcePackages` right after it configures the app. `flutterfire configure` writes the phase again, so after running it yourself on macOS, run in the directory of the app:

```bash
ruby -e 'f = ARGV[0]; s = File.exist?(f) ? File.binread(f) : ""; t = s.gsub("$BUILD_DIR/SourcePackages/checkouts/firebase-ios-sdk/Crashlytics/run", "$SRCROOT/../build/ios/SourcePackages/checkouts/firebase-ios-sdk/Crashlytics/run"); if t == s then puts "Nothing to fix in #{f}" else File.binwrite(f, t); puts "Fixed the Crashlytics phase in #{f}" end' ios/Runner.xcodeproj/project.pbxproj
```

## Texts

The texts of the app are in the ARB files of `lib/l10n`, one for each language. `lib/l10n/app_en.arb` has every text in English, and the file of another language, such as `app_uk.arb`, has the translations into it. [gen-l10n](https://docs.flutter.dev/ui/internationalization) of Flutter generates the `AppLocalizations` class from these files, as `l10n.yaml` says, and writes it into `app_localizations.dart` and a file for each language in the same directory. Run `flutter gen-l10n` after every change of the ARB files. In a fresh clone of the app, `flutter pub get` generates those files, so you can commit them or leave them out of the repository. Code reads a text as `context.l10n.<name>` with `lib/core/l10n/l10n.dart` imported, where `context` is a `BuildContext` below the root of the app.

To add a text, add it to `lib/l10n/app_en.arb` under a name of its own, and its translations under the same name to the files of the other languages. Then run `flutter gen-l10n`. A text without a translation into a language reads in English there.

The texts of a new language, such as German, go into a file of their own:

1. Add `lib/l10n/app_de.arb` with `"@@locale": "de"` and the translations.
2. Run `flutter gen-l10n`.

The section "Languages" tells where else the new language goes.

## flutter_local_notifications

The app shows its notifications with [flutter_local_notifications](https://pub.dev/packages/flutter_local_notifications): `lib/core/notifications/local_notifications_service.dart` implements the notifications service of the app on it (see the section Notifications).

The module set up what the package needs in the native projects:

- `android/app/build.gradle.kts` turns on core library desugaring with `desugar_jdk_libs` 2.1.4, without which the Android build fails.
- `android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml` declares the receivers of the package that show a scheduled notification at its time and set the scheduled ones again after a restart of the device or an update of the app, and the permission `android.permission.RECEIVE_BOOT_COMPLETED` for the restart.
- `ios/Runner/AppDelegate.swift` makes the app delegate the delegate of the notification center of iOS, so that a notification shows while the app is in the foreground and a tap reaches the app, also the tap that launched it.

Every notification goes to the channel `default`, with a high importance, which the service creates when the app starts. A scheduled notification uses an inexact alarm on Android, which needs no permission: Android may show it a few minutes late. To show notifications to the minute, declare `android.permission.SCHEDULE_EXACT_ALARM` in the manifest, ask for it with `requestExactAlarmsPermission()` of `AndroidFlutterLocalNotificationsPlugin`, and schedule with `AndroidScheduleMode.exactAllowWhileIdle`. Google Play allows exact alarms only for apps whose main feature needs them, such as an alarm clock or a calendar.

The app does not set up the actions of a notification, its buttons. They need the receiver `ActionBroadcastReceiver` on Android and, on iOS, a callback that registers the plugins for the isolate in which an action runs: see the README of the package.

## Sign-in

The sign-in mode of the app says who may use it without an account. This app was generated in the mode `required`: `authMode` in `lib/core/auth/app_session.dart` is `AuthMode.required`.

- In `required`, nobody may. `appSession.allowsApp` is true only for a user who signed in to an account.
- In `guest`, everyone may. The app has no user until someone signs in, and `appSession.allowsApp` is always true.
- In `anonymous`, everyone may, as an anonymous user that the app signs in itself when it starts. Code then has the id of a user, `appSession.value.uid`, before anyone has an account, and signing up gives that same user the account. The app waits up to 3 seconds for that sign-in. When it fails or takes longer, the app starts without a user and tries again each time the user comes back to it. `appSession.allowsApp` is always true.

The mode keeps nobody from a screen on its own. A guard of the routes does. One that reads `appSession.allowsApp` stands before the whole app. One that reads `appSession.hasAccount` stands only before the routes that it lists, the screens that need an account. A module with the screens of sign-in declares such guards. If the app was generated without such a module, every screen is open in every mode, `required` too, until you write a guard of your own that reads `appSession.allowsApp`. A screen that a module made for users with an account is open then too. A warning named each such route when the app was generated, and a guard of your own for them reads `appSession.hasAccount`.

To change the mode, change the value of `authMode`. The provider of sign-in has to allow what the new mode needs, such as anonymous users for the mode `anonymous`. Where the provider has a section in this README, that section tells how.

When an anonymous user signs in to an account that exists already, the user of the app changes, and what the app keeps under the id of the anonymous user would be left behind. `takeGuestData()` in `lib/core/auth/guest_data.dart` says what moves to the account. As generated it moves nothing.

Besides an email address and a password, the app signs in with these identity providers, the ways to sign in of `appSignInMethods` in `lib/core/auth/sign_in_methods.dart`:

- Continue with Google, on Android and iOS.
- Continue with Apple, on iOS. Deleting an account that signs in this way asks the identity provider for a credential again first, so that the provider of sign-in can revoke the tokens that it gave the app. If the user closes its sheet, nothing is deleted.

A screen of sign-in shows the button of a way to sign in only on the platforms that it works on. When an anonymous user signs in this way and no account has the identity yet, the user gets the identity and keeps the id. Otherwise the user signs in to that account, as with a password, and `takeGuestData()` moves what the anonymous user had. The sign-in may also fail, because the address of the identity has an account that signs in in another way: the provider of sign-in decides which.

## Sign in with Apple

The app signs in with Apple on iOS: `appleCredential()` in `lib/core/apple_sign_in/apple_sign_in.dart` asks Apple for a credential with the sheet of Sign in with Apple, and a screen of sign-in shows the button of Apple on iOS only. On Android the app has no Sign in with Apple.

Sign in with Apple is a capability of the app. The key `com.apple.developer.applesignin` in `ios/Runner/Runner.entitlements` asks for it, and the App ID of the app needs it too: when Xcode manages the signing of the app, it adds the capability to the App ID. Signing with the capability needs a team of the paid Apple Developer Program. A free personal team cannot sign the app for an iPhone: to run the app there, remove the key from `ios/Runner/Runner.entitlements`, and signing in with Apple then fails with the reason `notConfigured`. Without the capability, Apple answers with an unknown error, which fails with `notConfigured` too.

Apple gives the name of the user only the first time that the user signs in to the app: the credential has it then, and a later one has none. The user may also hide the email address. The account then gets an address of the private relay of Apple, which forwards to the user only the messages of senders that are registered with Apple. Register the domains or the addresses from which the app and its provider of sign-in send email, such as the message of a password reset, in your Apple Developer account, under Sign in with Apple for Email Communication.

The App Store asks an app with Sign in with Apple to revoke the tokens that Apple gave it when the user deletes the account (App Store Review Guideline 5.1.1(v)). So `appSession.deleteAccount()` asks Apple for a new authorization first, and the provider of sign-in revokes the tokens with its code. The backend of the app needs a key of Apple to revoke them, and the documentation of the provider of sign-in tells where to set it. If the user closes the sheet of Apple, nothing is deleted.

The icon of the button, `AppleSignInIcon` in `lib/core/apple_sign_in/apple_sign_in.dart`, is the Apple logo of the Material icons. Apple asks a custom button of Sign in with Apple to use the logo artwork of [Apple Design Resources](https://developer.apple.com/design/resources/), and App Review checks every such button. Before you submit the app, download that logo and show it in `AppleSignInIcon`.

## Google sign-in

The app signs in with Google on Android and iOS: `googleCredential()` in `lib/core/google_sign_in/google_sign_in.dart` asks Google for a credential with the sheet of Google sign-in. A sign-in screen of the app, such as the one of the `sign_in` module, shows the button of Google.

Google sign-in needs the Google project of the app to know the app, and the app to know the clients of Google:

- On Android, Google knows the app by the name of its package and the SHA-1 of each key that signs it: the debug key of each machine that builds the app, the upload key, and the app signing key of Google Play.
- On iOS, the app needs the id of its iOS client of Google, and the reversed id of that client as a URL scheme in `CFBundleURLTypes` of `ios/Runner/Info.plist`, through which the sheet of Google returns to the app.
- On both, Google issues the ID token to the web client of the Google project, the server client, against which the backend of the provider of sign-in checks the token.

`googleCredential()` starts google_sign_in with `GoogleSignIn.instance.initialize()` without ids, so google_sign_in reads them from the files of the app: on iOS `CLIENT_ID` of `GoogleService-Info.plist`, or `GIDClientID` of `ios/Runner/Info.plist`, and on Android the string resource `default_web_client_id`. The section of the provider of sign-in in this README tells which of these it sets up. To use ids of your own, pass them to `initialize()` in `googleCredential()` of `lib/core/google_sign_in/google_sign_in.dart`. Until Google sign-in is set up, signing in with Google fails with the reason `notConfigured`, and the `developerHint` of the failure tells what Google answered.

On iOS, the Google Sign-In SDK stops the app instead when the app has no iOS client of Google or not its URL scheme: google_sign_in does not catch the exception that the SDK throws then. Set both up before you try the button on iOS.

If the button of Google does nothing on Android, check that the SHA-1 of the key that signs the app is registered with Google: Credential Manager may answer a key that Google does not know as if the user had closed the sheet.

The icon of the button, `GoogleSignInIcon` in `lib/core/google_sign_in/google_sign_in.dart`, shows the "G" of Google from `lib/core/google_sign_in/assets/google_logo.png`, the logo of the Google Sign-In SDK (Apache License 2.0). The [branding guidelines of Sign in with Google](https://developers.google.com/identity/branding-guidelines) ask a custom button to show that logo in its own colours, on a light background, without a change: keep the image as it is.

## Values in .env

`.env` at the root of the app holds the values you fill in after generating the app, such as the public key of a service. Code reads each value as a static getter of `AppEnv` in `lib/core/env/app_env.dart`. The getter is `null` while the value is empty or missing, so the app builds and starts before you fill anything in.

`.gitignore` lists `.env`, so it stays out of git. `.env.example` is in git and has the same keys without values: after a clone, run `cp .env.example .env` and fill in `.env`. Every value ends up in the app, where anyone can read it, so never put a secret there, such as the secret key of a server, a password or a private key.

The keys of the app:

- `REVENUECAT_APPLE_KEY`, read as `AppEnv.revenuecatAppleKey`, from the module `revenuecat`: The public API key of the App Store app in RevenueCat, which starts with appl_
- `REVENUECAT_GOOGLE_KEY`, read as `AppEnv.revenuecatGoogleKey`, from the module `revenuecat`: The public API key of the Play Store app in RevenueCat, which starts with goog_
- `REVENUECAT_TEST_KEY`, read as `AppEnv.revenuecatTestKey`, from the module `revenuecat`: The public API key of the Test Store in RevenueCat, which starts with test_, for debug builds only
- `PAYWALL_TERMS_URL`, read as `AppEnv.paywallTermsUrl`, from the module `paywall`: The address of the terms of use that the paywall links to
- `PAYWALL_PRIVACY_URL`, read as `AppEnv.paywallPrivacyUrl`, from the module `paywall`: The address of the privacy policy that the paywall links to

To add a key of your own, put it into `.env.example` and `.env` and give `AppEnv` a getter for it. The section of the module that provides the values tells how they get into the app.

## RevenueCat

The app sells its premium access with [RevenueCat](https://www.revenuecat.com) and [purchases_flutter](https://pub.dev/packages/purchases_flutter): `lib/core/purchases/revenuecat_purchases_service.dart` implements the purchases service of the app on it (see the section Purchases). The premium access is the entitlement `premium` in RevenueCat, and the plans of the app are the packages of the current offering. Every package of that offering unlocks the same access, so attach each of its products to `premium`.

To buy a plan right after generating the app, with no account in a store:

1. Create a project in RevenueCat. A new project has a Test Store with products.
2. Create an entitlement with the identifier `premium` and attach the products to it.
3. Add the packages of those products to an offering, and make that offering the current one.
4. Copy the public API key of the Test Store, which starts with `test_`, into `REVENUECAT_TEST_KEY` in `.env`.
5. Run a debug build as the section Values in .env says, and buy a plan. The Test Store shows a dialog of its own, where "Test valid purchase" completes the purchase.

For a release, add the App Store app and the Play Store app to the project in RevenueCat. Give RevenueCat the In-App Purchase key of App Store Connect and a service account of Google Play, so that it checks the purchases in the stores. Create the products in the stores, attach them to `premium` and add their packages to the offering. Then copy the public API keys of the two apps into `REVENUECAT_APPLE_KEY` (`appl_`) and `REVENUECAT_GOOGLE_KEY` (`goog_`). The section Finish the setup lists these steps.

A debug build takes `REVENUECAT_TEST_KEY` when it is filled in, and the key of the store of the platform otherwise. A profile or release build never takes it: RevenueCat stops such a build that is configured with a key of the Test Store, on purpose. So to try the sandbox of a store in a debug build, leave `REVENUECAT_TEST_KEY` empty.

These keys are the public API keys of RevenueCat, which go into the app, where anyone can read them. A secret API key of RevenueCat, which starts with `sk_`, never goes into `.env`, and the app refuses one. While the key that a build takes has no value, the app runs without purchases: it never calls RevenueCat, every call of `appPurchases` fails with the reason `notConfigured`, and a debug build prints which key to fill in.

## Languages

The app is in the languages of `appLocales` in `lib/core/l10n/app_locale.dart`. It shows its texts in the language that the device prefers among them, and in the first of the list when the device asks for none of them. `appLocale.choose()` puts the app into another of its languages, whatever the device prefers, and the app remembers that choice between its launches. `appLocale.choose(null)` follows the device again. The entry `LanguageSetting` of the settings screen, in `lib/core/l10n/language_setting.dart`, lets the user choose.

To add a language in which Flutter has the texts of its own widgets, such as German, add the texts of the app in German, and then:

1. Add `Locale('de')` to `appLocales`, one locale for each language, since the app tells its languages apart by their codes.
2. Add `de` to `CFBundleLocalizations` in `ios/Runner/Info.plist`, which tells iOS the languages of the app.
3. Add `'de': 'Deutsch'` to `_names` in `lib/core/l10n/language_setting.dart`, the name that the setting shows for the language. Without it, the setting shows `de`.

## Notifications

The app shows notifications through `appNotifications` in `lib/core/notifications/app_notifications.dart`. `show()` shows one now and `schedule()` at a time to come, `cancel()` and `cancelAll()` remove them, and `scheduled()` lists those still to come. A scheduled notification shows at about its time, not to the minute, even if the app is closed by then, and after the device restarts. A tap on a notification reaches `appNotifications.taps` with the `data` of the notification, and `appNotifications.launchTap` is the tap that launched the app.

The app needs the permission of the user to show notifications. `appNotifications.permission` is `granted`, `denied` once the user said no or turned them off, or `notAsked`, and `appNotifications.request()` asks. Where the system has no question to show, as on Android before 13, the request opens the settings of the system for the notifications of the app instead. The onboarding asks on a page of its own, with the functions of `lib/core/notifications/notifications_step.dart`. A user who skips the onboarding does not see that page. The entry `NotificationsSetting` of the settings screen, in `lib/core/notifications/notifications_setting.dart`, shows whether the app may show notifications. A tap asks while the app has not asked, and opens the settings of the system after that. After `denied` the system may not ask again: iOS asks only once, and Android stops asking after a second no. `appNotifications.openSettings()` opens the settings of the system, where the user turns the notifications on.

On Android a notification shows `android/app/src/main/res/drawable/ic_notification.xml` as its small icon. Replace the bell there with the icon of the app, a shape on a transparent background, and keep the name of the file: `android/app/src/main/res/raw/notifications_keep.xml` keeps the icon in a release build. In the settings of the system, the channel of the notifications has the name of the app.

## Firebase Cloud Messaging

The app receives its push messages with [firebase_messaging](https://pub.dev/packages/firebase_messaging): `lib/core/push/firebase_push_service.dart` implements the push service of the app on it (see the section Push messages). The address of this device is its registration token of Firebase Cloud Messaging.

Firebase sends messages to iOS through the Apple Push Notification service (APNs), with an APNs key of your team, which you upload by hand once for the project:

1. In your Apple Developer account, under Certificates, Identifiers & Profiles, create a key under Keys with the Apple Push Notifications service (APNs) enabled. Download its `.p8` file, and note its Key ID and your Team ID.
2. In the Firebase console, open the settings of the project, then Cloud Messaging, and upload the key under the configuration of the Apple app, with its Key ID and your Team ID.

A key sends only to the apps of its team, so register the bundle id of the app as an App ID in that team, with Push Notifications. The key matters only for sending: the iOS app gets its address without it, but Firebase cannot send to that address. The section Finish the setup lists the upload among the steps that you do by hand.

To send this device a test message, run the app in debug mode and copy the address that it prints. In the Firebase console, open Messaging, create a notification campaign, choose Send test message and paste the address. A server sends a message with the HTTP v1 API of Firebase Cloud Messaging: `POST https://fcm.googleapis.com/v1/projects/<project-id>/messages:send`, with an OAuth token of a service account of the project and the address as the `token` of the message.

`android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml` names the channel `default` of the notifications of the app (`com.google.firebase.messaging.default_notification_channel_id`) and their small icon `@drawable/ic_notification` (`com.google.firebase.messaging.default_notification_icon`). Android shows a message that arrives in the background with them.

On iOS, firebase_messaging answers the system for every notification that arrives while the app is in the foreground, the notifications that the app shows itself too. So the service sets its presentation options to an alert, a badge and a sound when the app starts (`setForegroundNotificationPresentationOptions()`): without them, iOS would show none of these notifications in the foreground.

The app runs no code for a message that arrives in the background. To handle one, register a top-level function with `FirebaseMessaging.onBackgroundMessage()` in `main()`. It runs in an isolate of its own, without what `bootstrap()` sets up.

## Purchases

The app sells premium access through `appPurchases` in `lib/core/purchases/app_purchases.dart`. Every plan of the current offer gives the same access, whether it renews or the user pays once.

`appPurchases.value` is `NoAccess` or `PremiumAccess`, and `appPurchases.hasPremium` tells which. The app knows it before the first frame, from what the device keeps. `PremiumAccess` tells when the access ends (`expiresAt`, `null` for an access that never ends), whether the plan `renews`, whether the access is a free trial (`inTrial`), and `manageUrl`, the page of the store where the user manages or cancels the plan. `appPurchases.plans()` gives the plans to sell, `appPurchases.purchase(plan)` buys one and returns `false` when the user cancels, and `appPurchases.restore()` gives the user what the store account bought. A call fails only with a `PurchasesFailure`: show a text of the app for its `reason`, and its `developerHint` in debug mode only.

A screen that sells a plan shows what the App Store and Google Play ask of it:
- the name of the plan, how long a period lasts and what the user gets;
- the full price that the store charges for each period, shown more clearly than any other price on the screen, and for a yearly plan never mainly as a price per month;
- for a free trial or a lower price at first, how long it lasts and the price after it;
- that the plan renews until the user cancels, and how to cancel it in the store;
- a way to restore purchases;
- links to the terms of use and the privacy policy of the app;
- a visible way to close the screen without buying.

A screen that only users with premium access may see stands behind a guard of the routes that reads `appPurchases.hasPremium`. Without such a guard, every user gets to it.

The purchases belong to the user of `appSession`, by the id of the user: the user of an account, or an anonymous user. While nobody is signed in, they belong to the user of this device. A user who buys before signing up keeps the purchase in the new account, which takes it over from the user of this device, and an anonymous user keeps it when the account takes the id of that user. A user who signs in to an account that exists already leaves the purchases with the user of this device. After a sign-out, the user of this device is a new one, with nothing bought. In both cases a purchase comes back only through a restore, which may also move purchases from another user, as the backend of the provider decides. Without a restore, the next user of the device never gets the purchases of a user who signed out. The entry `RestorePurchasesSetting` of the settings screen, in `lib/core/purchases/restore_purchases_setting.dart`, restores purchases.

Until the provider of purchases has what it needs, such as its keys, the app runs without purchases: `appPurchases.value` stays `NoAccess`, every call fails with the reason `notConfigured`, whose `developerHint` names what is missing, and in debug mode the app prints that hint once.

## Paywall

The paywall is at `/paywall`, in `lib/features/paywall/`. It shows the plans of the current offer of the purchases of the app (see the section Purchases): a card for each plan, with the price that the store gives, how often the plan renews and its free trial or lower price at first. A yearly plan that costs less than twelve months of a monthly plan in the same currency is the best value. The button buys the selected plan, and the paywall restores purchases and links to your terms of use and your privacy policy.

The screen of the subscription, at `/paywall/subscription`, is only for a user with premium access. It shows the plan, when it renews or ends, and a button to the page of the store where the user manages or cancels it. A request for it from a user without the access opens the paywall over the page first. In an app with a settings screen, its entry Subscription leads there. In an app without one, open it from a screen of yours with `context.nav.paywall.subscription().push<void>()`, so that the user finds where to cancel.

No screen of the paywall closes itself or navigates after a purchase. The route `/paywall/subscription` asks for premium access, and the guard `paywall.hasPremium` stands for it: once the user has the access, the router closes the paywall and shows the route. To sell from a button of yours, navigate to a route that needs premium access, as above. To keep a screen of your own for users with the access, list `PurchasesRole.premium` in the `conditions` of its route in a module, or add its full name to the `routes` of `paywall.hasPremium` in `routeGuards`. Do not navigate to `/paywall` itself for a user with premium access: the router shows the screen that the app starts on then.

The paywall shows what the App Store and Google Play ask of a screen that sells a plan: the full price of each plan most clearly, the length of a free trial and the price after it right below the button, that the plan renews until the user cancels it in the store, a way to restore purchases, the links to the terms of use and the privacy policy, and a close button that stays in sight. A plan paid once says that it is no subscription.

A text of the app has no parameters, so the paywall names the periods and the lengths of intro offers that the stores have: a plan that renews every week, every 1, 2, 3 or 6 months, or every year, and an intro offer of 3 days, 1 or 2 weeks, 1, 2, 3 or 6 months, or 1 year. A plan of another period shows the title of its product in the store, and an intro offer of another length is a free trial or an introductory price without its length. `lib/features/paywall/paywall_texts.dart` puts these texts together.

The benefits on the paywall are texts of the module, `benefitAll`, `benefitNew` and `benefitSupport`. Change them to what premium access gives in your app. In an app with localization, they are texts of the app, which `lib/features/paywall/paywall_view.dart` reads as `context.l10n.paywallBenefitAll`, `paywallBenefitNew` and `paywallBenefitSupport`: change them where the provider of the localization keeps the texts of the app, in each language. In an app without it, they are the English texts in that file.

Put the addresses of your terms of use and of your privacy policy into `PAYWALL_TERMS_URL` and `PAYWALL_PRIVACY_URL` in `.env` (see the section Values in .env). The App Store asks a paywall to link to both. While a value is empty or is no web address, the paywall leaves its link out, but on iOS the terms of use then lead to the standard license agreement of Apple. A debug build shows a note for you at the bottom of the paywall, which names the keys that are empty and what the provider of purchases says is missing, such as its key.

In an app with sign-in, a user may buy before signing in. A sign-up keeps the purchase in the new account, while a sign-in to an account that exists already leaves the purchase with the user of the device until the user restores it.

## Push messages

The app receives push messages through `appPush` in `lib/core/push/app_push.dart`.

`appPush.address` is the address of this device. The backend of the app sends its messages there. It is `null` until the platform gives one, and the app works without it. Send each new address to the backend: it may come late, and it may change. In debug mode the app prints it, so you can send this device a message by hand.

On iOS the address needs a token of the Apple Push Notification service (APNs). iOS gives one only to a build signed with the entitlement `aps-environment`, which `ios/Runner/Runner.entitlements` has. The iOS simulator on a Mac gets a token. One on a macOS virtual machine, such as a hosted CI runner, never does. On an iPhone, signing with the entitlement needs a team of the paid Apple Developer Program. With a free personal team, Xcode cannot sign the app: to run it on an iPhone, remove the key `aps-environment` from `ios/Runner/Runner.entitlements`, and the app runs there without an address. The simulator needs no change. The value `development` suits the builds that you run from Xcode. When Xcode exports the app for distribution, it takes the value of the provisioning profile (production for a distribution profile).

A message that arrives while the app is in the foreground reaches `appPush.messages`. One with a title or a body also shows as a notification, as it does in the background. Where the system does not show it, the app shows it with `appNotifications`. Either way the user has to allow the notifications of the app, as the section Notifications tells. In the background the system shows the message, on Android in the channel of the notifications of the app.

`appPush.taps` tells of each tap on a message while the app runs, and `appPush.launchMessage` is the message whose tap launched the app. Read both once the first screen is shown, and navigate as the rest of the app does. A tap on a message does not reach `appNotifications.taps`, so the code of the app sees each tap once.

A message with data and no title or body reaches the app only while it is in the foreground. In the background, or when the app is closed, it runs no code of the app. Images in a message do not show on iOS, which needs an extension of the app for them.

pluginManagement {
    val flutterSdkPath =
        run {
            val properties = java.util.Properties()
            file("local.properties").inputStream().use { properties.load(it) }
            val flutterSdkPath = properties.getProperty("flutter.sdk")
            require(flutterSdkPath != null) { "flutter.sdk not set in local.properties" }
            flutterSdkPath
        }

    includeBuild("$flutterSdkPath/packages/flutter_tools/gradle")

    repositories {
        google()
        mavenCentral()
        gradlePluginPortal()
    }
}

  override func application(
    _ application: UIApplication,

    return super.application(application, didFinishLaunchingWithOptions: launchOptions)

    GeneratedPluginRegistrant.register(with: engineBridge.pluginRegistry)
  }

import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:flutter_localizations/flutter_localizations.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/app/status_bar_icons.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/l10n/app_locale.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/theme/app_theme.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/theme/theme_mode.dart';
import 'package:my_app/l10n/app_localizations.dart';

import 'core/router/app_router.dart';

/// The root widget of the app.
class App extends StatelessWidget {
  /// Creates the root widget.
  const App({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) => MaterialApp.router(
    title: 'My App',
    theme: createLightTheme(context),
    darkTheme: createDarkTheme(context),
    themeMode: AppThemeModeScope.of(context),
    locale: AppLocaleScope.of(context),
    localizationsDelegates: [
      AppLocalizations.delegate,
      GlobalMaterialLocalizations.delegate,
      GlobalWidgetsLocalizations.delegate,
      GlobalCupertinoLocalizations.delegate,
    ],
    supportedLocales: [...appLocales],
    routerConfig: appRouter.config,
    builder: (context, child) => StatusBarIcons(child: child!),
  );
}
=== lib/bootstrap.dart (flutter_core) ===
import 'package:firebase_core/firebase_core.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/auth/app_session.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/crash_reporting/crash_reporter.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/di/dependencies.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/notifications/app_notifications.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/preferences/app_preferences.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/purchases/app_purchases.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/push/app_push.dart';
import 'package:my_app/firebase_options.dart';

/// Runs the start-up code of the app before its first frame: first what
/// must come before anything else, then the platform services, then the
/// services of the app, then what needs them.
Future<void> bootstrap() async {
  await Firebase.initializeApp(options: DefaultFirebaseOptions.currentPlatform);
  await initAuth();
  installCrashReporting();
  await initPreferences();
  await initNotifications();
  await initPurchases();
  await initPush();
  await registerDependencies();
}
=== lib/core/analytics/analytics_service.dart (role:analytics) ===
import 'package:flutter/foundation.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/analytics/firebase_analytics_service.dart' as impl0;

/// Records what users do in the app.
abstract interface class AnalyticsService {
  /// Logs the event [name] with [parameters].
  Future<void> logEvent(String name, {Map<String, Object>? parameters});

  /// Logs that the user signed in, with the sign-in [method].
  Future<void> logSignIn({String? method, Map<String, Object>? parameters});

  /// Logs that the user signed up, with the sign-up [method].
  Future<void> logSignUp({
    required String method,
    Map<String, Object>? parameters,
  });

  /// Turns the collection of analytics data on or off.
  Future<void> setAnalyticsCollectionEnabled(bool enabled);

  /// Sets the id of the signed-in user, or clears it with `null`.
  Future<void> setUserId(String? userId);

  /// Sets the user property [name] to [value], or clears it with `null`.
  Future<void> setUserProperty({required String name, required String? value});
}

/// Returns the analytics service of the app, which forwards every call to
/// the analytics services of all modules.
///
/// It calls each of them on its own, and its future completes once all of
/// them are done. An analytics service that throws, or whose future fails,
/// keeps no other from the call, and its failure does not reach the code
/// that called: that code may not await the call, and then nothing would
/// catch the failure. In debug mode the failure is printed, so that an
/// analytics service that does not work, such as one that is not set up,
/// shows in the console. Each analytics service gets a copy of its own of
/// the map of parameters, so that one that changes the map changes nothing
/// that the caller or another analytics service has. An analytics service
/// whose factory throws, or that fails to start, is left out, and in debug
/// mode its error is printed.
AnalyticsService createAnalyticsService() => _analyticsService;

final AnalyticsService _analyticsService = _AnalyticsServices(
  _analyticsServices,
);

final List<AnalyticsService> _analyticsServices = [
  ?_createAlone(
    'createFirebaseAnalyticsService',
    impl0.createFirebaseAnalyticsService,
  ),
];

/// Calls [create], the function [name], on its own: returns what it
/// creates, or `null` if it throws, so that the app works without it, and
/// in debug mode prints the error.
AnalyticsService? _createAlone(
  String name,
  AnalyticsService Function() create,
) {
  try {
    return create();
  } on Object catch (error) {
    if (kDebugMode) {
      debugPrint('$name() failed, so the app works without it: $error');
    }
    return null;
  }
}

final class _AnalyticsServices implements AnalyticsService {
  const _AnalyticsServices(this._services);

  final List<AnalyticsService> _services;

  @override
  Future<void> logEvent(String name, {Map<String, Object>? parameters}) =>
      _forAll(
        (service) => service.logEvent(name, parameters: _copyOf(parameters)),
      );

  @override
  Future<void> logSignIn({String? method, Map<String, Object>? parameters}) =>
      _forAll(
        (service) =>
            service.logSignIn(method: method, parameters: _copyOf(parameters)),
      );

  @override
  Future<void> logSignUp({
    required String method,
    Map<String, Object>? parameters,
  }) => _forAll(
    (service) =>
        service.logSignUp(method: method, parameters: _copyOf(parameters)),
  );

  @override
  Future<void> setAnalyticsCollectionEnabled(bool enabled) =>
      _forAll((service) => service.setAnalyticsCollectionEnabled(enabled));

  @override
  Future<void> setUserId(String? userId) =>
      _forAll((service) => service.setUserId(userId));

  @override
  Future<void> setUserProperty({
    required String name,
    required String? value,
  }) => _forAll((service) => service.setUserProperty(name: name, value: value));

  /// Calls [call] with each analytics service on its own, and completes
  /// once all of them are done, whatever each does; see
  /// [createAnalyticsService].
  Future<void> _forAll(
    Future<void> Function(AnalyticsService service) call,
  ) async {
    await Future.wait([
      for (final service in _services) _callAlone(service, call),
    ]);
  }

  /// A copy of [parameters] for one analytics service; see
  /// [createAnalyticsService].
  static Map<String, Object>? _copyOf(Map<String, Object>? parameters) =>
      parameters == null ? null : Map.of(parameters);

  /// Calls [call] with [service], and keeps what it throws, or the error of
  /// its future, from the caller: in debug mode it prints it.
  static Future<void> _callAlone(
    AnalyticsService service,
    Future<void> Function(AnalyticsService service) call,
  ) async {
    try {
      await call(service);
    } on Object catch (error) {
      if (kDebugMode) {
        debugPrint(
          'The analytics service ${service.runtimeType} failed: $error',
        );
      }
    }
  }
}
=== lib/core/analytics/firebase_analytics_service.dart (firebase_analytics) ===
import 'package:firebase_analytics/firebase_analytics.dart';
import 'package:flutter/foundation.dart' show debugPrint;
import 'package:flutter/services.dart' show PlatformException;

import 'analytics_service.dart';

/// Creates an analytics service that records to Firebase Analytics, which
/// needs Firebase to be initialized.
///
/// The app has one analytics service, which `createAnalyticsService()`
/// returns and which forwards every call to this one.
AnalyticsService createFirebaseAnalyticsService() =>
    FirebaseAnalyticsService(FirebaseAnalytics.instance);

/// Records what users do in the app with Firebase Analytics.
///
/// A sign-in and a sign-up are the standard `login` and `sign_up` events of
/// Firebase Analytics, with the method in their `method` parameter.
final class FirebaseAnalyticsService implements AnalyticsService {
  /// Creates the service on the given [FirebaseAnalytics].
  FirebaseAnalyticsService(this._analytics);

  final FirebaseAnalytics _analytics;

  @override
  Future<void> logEvent(String name, {Map<String, Object>? parameters}) =>
      _analytics.logEvent(name: name, parameters: parameters);

  @override
  Future<void> logSignIn({String? method, Map<String, Object>? parameters}) =>
      _analytics.logLogin(loginMethod: method, parameters: parameters);

  @override
  Future<void> logSignUp({
    required String method,
    Map<String, Object>? parameters,
  }) => _analytics.logSignUp(signUpMethod: method, parameters: parameters);

  @override
  Future<void> setAnalyticsCollectionEnabled(bool enabled) =>
      _analytics.setAnalyticsCollectionEnabled(enabled);

  @override
  Future<void> setUserId(String? userId) => _analytics.setUserId(id: userId);

  @override
  Future<void> setUserProperty({
    required String name,
    required String? value,
  }) => _analytics.setUserProperty(name: name, value: value);
}

/// Logs a screen view to Firebase Analytics for the screen the user sees,
/// which the router of the app tells it about, named by the full name of
/// its route, such as `home.home`.
///
/// A screen that is no route of a module is the fallback start screen of
/// the app, logged as `/`, or the error screen of the router, which is not
/// logged: the location that the app cannot show may hold anything, such
/// as a token of a link.
///
/// Nothing waits for the screen view, so an error of the platform is
/// printed rather than left to the handler of the uncaught errors of the
/// app.
void logFirebaseScreenView(String? route, String location) {
  final name = route ?? (Uri.parse(location).path == '/' ? '/' : null);
  if (name == null) return;
  FirebaseAnalytics.instance
      .logScreenView(screenName: name)
      .catchError(
        (Object error) => debugPrint('Firebase Analytics: $error'),
        test: (error) => error is PlatformException,
      );
}
=== lib/core/app/status_bar_icons.dart (role:app_entry) ===
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:flutter/services.dart';

/// Asks the system for the icons of the status bar that suit the theme,
/// around [child]: dark icons on a light theme, light ones on a dark theme.
///
/// The root of the app puts it around the content of every route, below
/// the `MaterialApp` whose theme it reads. It leaves the navigation bar of
/// the system as the platform has it. A screen with an `AppBar` gets the
/// icons that suit its bar, since the bar tells them for the top of the
/// screen itself.
class StatusBarIcons extends StatelessWidget {
  /// Creates the widget around [child].
  const StatusBarIcons({required this.child, super.key});

  /// What the icons suit: the content of a route.
  final Widget child;

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final brightness = Theme.of(context).brightness;
    return AnnotatedRegion<SystemUiOverlayStyle>(
      // iOS takes the brightness of the bar, and Android that of its icons.
      value: SystemUiOverlayStyle(
        statusBarBrightness: brightness,
        statusBarIconBrightness: brightness == Brightness.dark
            ? Brightness.light
            : Brightness.dark,
      ),
      child: child,
    );
  }
}
=== lib/core/apple_sign_in/apple_sign_in.dart (apple_sign_in) ===
import 'dart:convert';

import 'package:crypto/crypto.dart';
import 'package:flutter/foundation.dart';
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:sign_in_with_apple/sign_in_with_apple.dart';

import '../auth/app_session.dart';

/// The issuer of the ID tokens of Apple.
const String _issuer = 'https://appleid.apple.com';

/// Asks Apple for a credential with the sheet of Sign in with Apple, and
/// returns it, or `null` when the user closed the sheet.
///
/// The request asks for the email address and the full name of the user,
/// with the SHA-256 hash of a new random nonce, which Apple writes into the
/// ID token. The credential has the nonce itself, with which the provider
/// of sign-in checks the token, and the authorization code of this request,
/// with which the provider can revoke the tokens that Apple gave the app
/// when the account is deleted. Apple gives the name of the user only the
/// first time that the user signs in to the app, so a later credential has
/// none.
///
/// It works on iOS only. On another platform it fails with
/// `notConfigured` without asking Apple. Apple answers an app without the
/// capability Sign in with Apple with an unknown error, which fails with
/// `notConfigured` too, and with a hint at the section of the README of the
/// app on it, "Sign in with Apple". Any other error fails with `unknown`.
Future<SignInCredential?> appleCredential() async {
  if (defaultTargetPlatform != TargetPlatform.iOS) {
    throw AuthFailure(
      AuthFailureReason.notConfigured,
      developerHint:
          'Sign in with Apple works on iOS only, and the app runs on '
          '${defaultTargetPlatform.name}.',
    );
  }
  final rawNonce = generateNonce();
  final AuthorizationCredentialAppleID apple;
  try {
    apple = await SignInWithApple.getAppleIDCredential(
      scopes: const [
        AppleIDAuthorizationScopes.email,
        AppleIDAuthorizationScopes.fullName,
      ],
      nonce: sha256.convert(utf8.encode(rawNonce)).toString(),
    );
  } on SignInWithAppleAuthorizationException catch (error) {
    switch (error.code) {
      case AuthorizationErrorCode.canceled:
        return null;
      case AuthorizationErrorCode.unknown:
        throw AuthFailure(
          AuthFailureReason.notConfigured,
          developerHint:
              'Apple answered with an unknown error (${error.message}). It '
              'does so when the app is not signed with the capability Sign '
              'in with Apple, which needs a team of the paid Apple Developer '
              'Program, and may do so on a device without an Apple Account. '
              'See the section "Sign in with Apple" of the README of the app.',
        );
      default:
        throw AuthFailure(AuthFailureReason.unknown, developerHint: '$error');
    }
  } on Object catch (error) {
    throw AuthFailure(AuthFailureReason.unknown, developerHint: '$error');
  }
  final idToken = apple.identityToken;
  if (idToken == null) {
    throw const AuthFailure(
      AuthFailureReason.unknown,
      developerHint: 'Apple gave no identity token.',
    );
  }
  return IdTokenCredential(
    issuer: _issuer,
    idToken: idToken,
    rawNonce: rawNonce,
    authorizationCode: apple.authorizationCode,
    givenName: apple.givenName,
    familyName: apple.familyName,
  );
}

/// The icon of the button of Sign in with Apple: the Apple logo of the
/// Material icons, which takes the colour of the label of the button.
///
/// Apple asks a custom button of Sign in with Apple for its own logo
/// artwork, which Apple Design Resources has, and App Review checks every
/// such button: see the section of the README of the app on it,
/// "Sign in with Apple".
class AppleSignInIcon extends StatelessWidget {
  /// Creates the icon.
  const AppleSignInIcon({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) => const Icon(Icons.apple);
}
=== lib/core/auth/app_session.dart (role:auth) ===
import 'dart:async';

import 'package:flutter/foundation.dart';
import 'package:flutter/widgets.dart';
import 'package:my_app/core/auth/firebase_auth_service.dart' as impl0;

import 'auth_service.dart';
import 'guest_data.dart';
import 'sign_in_methods.dart';

export 'auth_service.dart'
    show AuthFailure, AuthFailureReason, IdTokenCredential, SignInCredential;

/// Who may use the app without an account.
enum AuthMode {
  /// Nobody: the user signs in first.
  required,

  /// Everyone. The app has no user until someone signs in, and asks for an
  /// account only where a screen needs one.
  guest,

  /// Everyone, as an anonymous user that the app signs in itself when it
  /// starts, so that code has the id of a user from the first frame. An
  /// account is asked for only where a screen needs one, and signing up
  /// gives it to that same user.
  anonymous,
}

/// The mode of this app, which was chosen when the app was generated.
///
/// The mode gates nothing on its own. It decides what [appSession] does when
/// it starts and what [AppSessionController.allowsApp] says, which a guard
/// of the routes reads.
///
/// To change the mode, change its value and keep the declaration as it is,
/// on a line that it starts: a tool of the app that cannot import this
/// file, which imports Flutter, reads the mode from this line.
const AuthMode authMode = AuthMode.required;

/// Who uses the app: nobody that the app knows, an anonymous user, or the
/// user of an account.
@immutable
sealed class AppSession {
  const AppSession();

  /// The id of the user, or `null` when nobody is signed in.
  String? get uid;
}

/// Nobody is signed in.
final class SignedOutSession extends AppSession {
  /// Creates the session of an app without a user.
  const SignedOutSession();

  @override
  String? get uid => null;

  @override
  bool operator ==(Object other) => other is SignedOutSession;

  @override
  int get hashCode => (SignedOutSession).hashCode;
}

/// An anonymous user is signed in: one without an account, whom the app
/// signed in itself.
final class AnonymousSession extends AppSession {
  /// Creates the session of the anonymous user [uid].
  const AnonymousSession(this.uid);

  @override
  final String uid;

  @override
  bool operator ==(Object other) =>
      other is AnonymousSession && other.uid == uid;

  @override
  int get hashCode => uid.hashCode;
}

/// The user of an account is signed in.
final class AccountSession extends AppSession {
  /// Creates the session of the account [uid].
  const AccountSession(this.uid, {this.email, this.issuers = const {}});

  @override
  final String uid;

  /// The email address of the account, or `null` if it has none, as an
  /// account of a way to sign in may not.
  final String? email;

  /// The identity providers that the account signs in with, each by the
  /// `issuer` of its `SignInMethod` in `appSignInMethods` of
  /// `sign_in_methods.dart`. It is empty for an account that signs in with
  /// a password alone.
  final Set<String> issuers;

  @override
  bool operator ==(Object other) =>
      other is AccountSession &&
      other.uid == uid &&
      other.email == email &&
      other.issuers.length == issuers.length &&
      other.issuers.containsAll(issuers);

  @override
  int get hashCode => Object.hash(uid, email, Object.hashAllUnordered(issuers));
}

/// The session of the app: who uses it, known before the first frame, and
/// the way to sign in, up and out.
///
/// Its `value` is the [AppSession], and it tells its listeners when that
/// changes. A widget follows it with a `ValueListenableBuilder`.
final appSession = AppSessionController(
  mode: authMode,
  takeGuestData: takeGuestData,
);

/// Keeps the session of the app up to date with the service of the provider
/// of sign-in, and signs in, up and out through that service. The app has
/// one, [appSession].
///
/// Every call here fails only with an [AuthFailure], whose reason the app
/// shows in a text of its own. An error of another kind becomes a failure
/// with the reason [AuthFailureReason.unknown] and the error as its
/// [AuthFailure.developerHint].
///
/// The calls run one after another, in the order they were made, and none
/// runs next to an anonymous sign-in that is on its way. So the user of the
/// app is the result of the call that was made last. In the mode
/// [AuthMode.anonymous], a call that leaves nobody signed in is followed by
/// an anonymous sign-in.
final class AppSessionController extends ChangeNotifier
    implements ValueListenable<AppSession> {
  /// Creates a controller for an app in [mode]. The app creates
  /// [appSession] and no other. A test creates one to see another mode, or
  /// a shorter [anonymousWait], and disposes of it.
  AppSessionController({
    required this.mode,
    required this.takeGuestData,
    this.anonymousWait = const Duration(seconds: 3),
  });

  /// Who may use the app without an account.
  final AuthMode mode;

  /// Takes what an anonymous user has before that user signs in to an
  /// account: `takeGuestData` of `guest_data.dart`.
  final Future<GiveGuestData?> Function(String guestUid) takeGuestData;

  /// How long the app waits for its anonymous user when it starts, and
  /// after a sign-out or the deletion of an account, in the mode
  /// [AuthMode.anonymous].
  final Duration anonymousWait;

  AuthService? _service;
  StreamSubscription<AuthUser?>? _userChanges;
  AppSession _session = const SignedOutSession();
  bool _disposed = false;

  /// Whether the app has started for the first time.
  bool _started = false;

  /// Completes at the first start. Only the first call made before it waits
  /// for it, and each later call waits for the call before it. That call
  /// creates it, so the start completes it in the zone of the call: created
  /// with the controller, it would complete in a microtask of the zone that
  /// read [appSession] first, which may run none any more, as the fake time
  /// of a widget test that is over before a later test starts the app in
  /// real time.
  Completer<void>? _untilStarted;

  /// The call that was made last, while a call is on its way: the next one
  /// waits for it. It is `null` when none is, and a call then awaits
  /// nothing. A future that completed in another zone would hold the call
  /// back in a widget test, where the app starts in real time and the test
  /// goes on in fake time.
  Future<void>? _lastCall;

  /// The anonymous sign-ins that are on their way, each by its service, so
  /// that the app starts no second one next to it.
  final Map<AuthService, Future<void>> _anonymousSignIns = Map.identity();

  /// Tells when the user comes back to the app, in the mode
  /// [AuthMode.anonymous].
  _ResumeObserver? _observer;

  /// Who uses the app.
  @override
  AppSession get value => _session;

  /// Whether the user may see the app: always in the modes [AuthMode.guest]
  /// and [AuthMode.anonymous], and with an account in the mode
  /// [AuthMode.required].
  ///
  /// It is computed from the session each time it is read, and its
  /// listeners are those of the session. So when the session changes, a
  /// listener finds this value and [hasAccount] both up to date, whichever
  /// of the two it listens to. A listener is also called for a change of
  /// the session that leaves this value as it was.
  late final ValueListenable<bool> allowsApp = _SessionFlag(
    this,
    (session) => mode != AuthMode.required || session is AccountSession,
  );

  /// Whether the user of an account is signed in, which an anonymous user
  /// is not. It follows the session as [allowsApp] does.
  late final ValueListenable<bool> hasAccount = _SessionFlag(
    this,
    (session) => session is AccountSession,
  );

  /// Starts the session with [service]: takes the user who is signed in on
  /// this device, and follows the changes that the service tells of.
  ///
  /// `initAuth()` calls it in `bootstrap()`, so the session is known before
  /// the first frame. In the mode [AuthMode.anonymous], an app without a
  /// user signs in anonymously here and waits for that for at most
  /// [anonymousWait]. When the sign-in fails or takes longer, the app
  /// starts without a user. A sign-in that takes longer still becomes the
  /// session when it comes, and the app tries again each time the user
  /// comes back to it.
  ///
  /// A call made before the first start waits for it: [signIn], [signUp],
  /// [signInWith], [sendPasswordReset], [signOut] and [deleteAccount]. Such
  /// calls run in
  /// their order once the start has the user, and the future of this start
  /// completes after them, but waits for no call that is made while it
  /// runs. The mocks of the tests of an app use this: a call of [signUp]
  /// made before `bootstrap()`, which nothing awaits, gives the app an
  /// account before its first frame. A test makes such a call outside the
  /// body of a test, as in a `setUpAll`: made in the body of a widget
  /// test, it waits in the fake time of that body, and a start that runs in
  /// real time never completes.
  ///
  /// It may be called again, with the service that the session has from
  /// then on: `initAuth()` does so in a test of the next launch of the app,
  /// and a test gives it a service of its own. Such a start takes its turn
  /// after the calls that are on their way.
  Future<void> start(AuthService service) async {
    // The calls that were made before this start. The first start lets
    // them run once it has the user, and a later one waits for them.
    final earlier = _lastCall;
    final first = !_started;
    if (!first) await earlier;
    unawaited(_userChanges?.cancel());
    _service = service;
    _userChanges = service.userChanges.listen(
      (_) => _sync(),
      onError: (Object error, StackTrace stackTrace) => _report(
        error,
        stackTrace,
        'while following the user of the provider of sign-in',
      ),
    );
    _sync();
    if (mode == AuthMode.anonymous) {
      if (_observer == null) {
        final observer = _observer = _ResumeObserver(_retry);
        WidgetsBinding.instance.addObserver(observer);
      }
      await _signInAnonymously();
    }
    if (!_started) {
      _started = true;
      _untilStarted?.complete();
    }
    if (first) await earlier;
  }

  /// Signs in to the account of [email] with [password].
  ///
  /// An anonymous user becomes the user of that account, and what the
  /// anonymous user had moves to the account as `takeGuestData` says.
  Future<void> signIn({required String email, required String password}) =>
      _call(
        (service) =>
            _toAccount(() => service.signIn(email: email, password: password)),
      );

  /// Creates an account for [email] with [password] and signs in to it.
  ///
  /// An anonymous user gets the account and keeps the id, so everything
  /// that the user had stays.
  Future<void> signUp({required String email, required String password}) =>
      _call(
        (service) => _session is AnonymousSession
            ? service.linkPassword(email: email, password: password)
            : service.signUp(email: email, password: password),
      );

  /// Signs in with [credential], which a way to sign in of
  /// `appSignInMethods` got from an identity provider.
  ///
  /// An anonymous user gets the identity of the credential and keeps the id,
  /// so everything that the user had stays. When that identity belongs to
  /// another user already, or its email address has an account that signs
  /// in in another way, the anonymous user signs in to that account
  /// instead, or the call fails with
  /// [AuthFailureReason.accountExistsWithOtherMethod], as the backend of the
  /// provider decides. What the anonymous user had moves to the account as
  /// `takeGuestData` says, as with [signIn].
  Future<void> signInWith(SignInCredential credential) => _call((
    service,
  ) async {
    if (_session is AnonymousSession) {
      try {
        await service.linkWith(credential);
        return;
      } on AuthFailure catch (failure) {
        if (failure.reason != AuthFailureReason.credentialInUse &&
            failure.reason != AuthFailureReason.accountExistsWithOtherMethod) {
          rethrow;
        }
      }
    }
    await _toAccount(() => service.signInWith(credential));
  });

  /// Sends the message with which the owner of the account of [email] sets
  /// a new password. It completes whether [email] has an account or not.
  Future<void> sendPasswordReset(String email) =>
      _call((service) => service.sendPasswordReset(email));

  /// Signs the user out. In the mode [AuthMode.anonymous], the app then
  /// signs in anonymously again, as when it starts.
  Future<void> signOut() =>
      _call((service) => service.signOut(), waitsForAnonymousUser: true);

  /// Deletes the user who is signed in. The app is then without a user, as
  /// after [signOut], and in the mode [AuthMode.anonymous] it signs in
  /// anonymously again.
  ///
  /// For an account that signs in with a way to sign in that
  /// `revokesOnDeletion` and is available on this platform, it first asks
  /// that way for a credential again, such as with the sheet of its
  /// identity provider, so that the provider of sign-in can revoke the
  /// tokens of the app. When the user closes the sheet, the call completes
  /// and deletes nothing, which is no failure.
  ///
  /// It may fail with [AuthFailureReason.recentSignInRequired]: the user
  /// then signs out, signs in again and repeats it.
  Future<void> deleteAccount() => _call((service) async {
    final revoking = _revokingMethod();
    if (revoking == null) {
      await service.deleteAccount();
      return;
    }
    final fresh = await revoking.credential();
    if (fresh != null) await service.deleteAccount(fresh: fresh);
  }, waitsForAnonymousUser: true);

  /// Stops following the service and the lifecycle of the app. A call that
  /// is on its way still ends as it would have, but tells no listener, and
  /// no anonymous sign-in follows it.
  @override
  void dispose() {
    _disposed = true;
    unawaited(_userChanges?.cancel());
    if (_observer case final observer?) {
      WidgetsBinding.instance.removeObserver(observer);
    }
    super.dispose();
  }

  /// The way to sign in of the account of the session that asks for a
  /// credential again before the account is deleted and is available on
  /// this platform, or `null` if it has none: the first of
  /// `appSignInMethods` that does.
  SignInMethod? _revokingMethod() => switch (_session) {
    AccountSession(:final issuers) =>
      appSignInMethods
          .where(
            (method) =>
                method.revokesOnDeletion &&
                method.isAvailable &&
                issuers.contains(method.issuer),
          )
          .firstOrNull,
    _ => null,
  };

  /// The user came back to the app. In the mode [AuthMode.anonymous], an
  /// app without a user then signs in anonymously again: as what follows
  /// every call, so this takes a turn among the calls with nothing else to
  /// do, and starts no anonymous sign-in next to a sign-in.
  void _retry() => unawaited(_call((service) async {}));

  /// Runs [call] with the service once the calls made before it are over,
  /// and not before the app has started for the first time.
  ///
  /// In the mode [AuthMode.anonymous], a call that leaves nobody signed in
  /// is followed by an anonymous sign-in. A call that
  /// [waitsForAnonymousUser], as a sign-out, completes once that sign-in is
  /// over or took too long, as the start does.
  Future<void> _call(
    Future<void> Function(AuthService service) call, {
    bool waitsForAnonymousUser = false,
  }) async {
    final earlier =
        _lastCall ??
        (_started ? null : (_untilStarted ??= Completer<void>()).future);
    final done = Completer<void>();
    final own = _lastCall = done.future;
    if (earlier != null) await earlier;
    try {
      await _guarded(call);
      final signIn = _signInAnonymously();
      if (waitsForAnonymousUser) await signIn;
    } finally {
      if (identical(_lastCall, own)) _lastCall = null;
      done.complete();
    }
  }

  /// Runs [call] with the service, once an anonymous sign-in that is on its
  /// way is over: of a call and a sign-in that run at the same time, the
  /// one that ended last would decide who the user is.
  ///
  /// Whatever [call] throws that is no [AuthFailure] becomes one, and the
  /// session has the user of the service when it is over, also when it
  /// failed. A call that failed does not wait for the anonymous sign-in
  /// that follows it, so that its failure is not late.
  Future<void> _guarded(Future<void> Function(AuthService service) call) async {
    final service = _service!;
    try {
      await _anonymousSignIns[service];
      await call(service);
    } on Object catch (error, stackTrace) {
      _sync();
      unawaited(_signInAnonymously());
      if (error is AuthFailure) rethrow;
      Error.throwWithStackTrace(
        AuthFailure(AuthFailureReason.unknown, developerHint: '$error'),
        stackTrace,
      );
    }
    _sync();
  }

  /// Signs in to an account with [signIn], in whichever way, and moves what
  /// an anonymous user had to that account, when it has another id.
  ///
  /// It takes the data before the sign-in, while the anonymous user is
  /// still signed in, and gives it once the user of another id has an
  /// account. If taking fails, the call fails and nothing has changed. If
  /// giving fails, the user is signed in already, so the call completes and
  /// the error is reported as an error of the app.
  Future<void> _toAccount(Future<void> Function() signIn) async {
    final guest = _session;
    final give = guest is AnonymousSession
        ? await takeGuestData(guest.uid)
        : null;
    await signIn();
    _sync();
    final account = _session;
    if (give == null ||
        account is! AccountSession ||
        account.uid == guest.uid) {
      return;
    }
    try {
      await give(account.uid);
    } on Object catch (error, stackTrace) {
      _report(
        error,
        stackTrace,
        'while giving the data of a guest to the account',
      );
    }
  }

  /// Reports [error], which no call can fail with, as an error of the app.
  void _report(Object error, StackTrace stackTrace, String during) =>
      FlutterError.reportError(
        FlutterErrorDetails(
          exception: error,
          stack: stackTrace,
          library: 'app session',
          context: ErrorDescription(during),
        ),
      );

  /// In the mode [AuthMode.anonymous], signs in anonymously when nobody is
  /// signed in, and waits for that for at most [anonymousWait].
  ///
  /// It never fails. A sign-in that takes longer goes on and becomes the
  /// session when it comes. One that fails leaves the app without a user
  /// until the next try, and in debug mode its error is printed.
  Future<void> _signInAnonymously() async {
    final service = _service!;
    if (_disposed ||
        mode != AuthMode.anonymous ||
        service.currentUser != null) {
      return;
    }
    final signIn = _anonymousSignIns[service] ??= _tryAnonymously(service)
        .whenComplete(() {
          _anonymousSignIns.remove(service);
          _sync();
        });
    await signIn.timeout(anonymousWait, onTimeout: () {});
  }

  Future<void> _tryAnonymously(AuthService service) async {
    try {
      await service.signInAnonymously();
    } on Object catch (error) {
      if (kDebugMode) {
        debugPrint(
          'The anonymous sign-in failed, so the app has no user: $error',
        );
      }
    }
  }

  /// Takes the user of the service as the session, and tells the listeners
  /// when that changes it, unless the session was disposed of.
  void _sync() {
    if (_disposed) return;
    final session = switch (_service!.currentUser) {
      null => const SignedOutSession(),
      AuthUser(isAnonymous: true, :final uid) => AnonymousSession(uid),
      AuthUser(:final uid, :final email, :final issuers) => AccountSession(
        uid,
        email: email,
        issuers: Set.unmodifiable(issuers),
      ),
    };
    if (session == _session) return;
    _session = session;
    notifyListeners();
  }
}

/// Calls [_onResume] each time the app is resumed, whatever state it was in
/// before.
final class _ResumeObserver with WidgetsBindingObserver {
  _ResumeObserver(this._onResume);

  final VoidCallback _onResume;

  @override
  void didChangeAppLifecycleState(AppLifecycleState state) {
    if (state == AppLifecycleState.resumed) _onResume();
  }
}

/// A flag of the session of a controller: its value is computed from the
/// session each time it is read, and its listeners are those of the
/// session.
final class _SessionFlag implements ValueListenable<bool> {
  const _SessionFlag(this._controller, this._of);

  final AppSessionController _controller;
  final bool Function(AppSession session) _of;

  @override
  bool get value => _of(_controller.value);

  @override
  void addListener(VoidCallback listener) => _controller.addListener(listener);

  @override
  void removeListener(VoidCallback listener) =>
      _controller.removeListener(listener);
}

/// Returns the service of the provider of sign-in, which [initAuth] created:
/// `bootstrap()` awaits it before the first frame, and there is none to
/// return before that.
///
